On three-dimensional flows of thermo-viscoelastic fluids of Giesekus type Note: Miroslav Bulíček and Tomáš Los supported by the project No. 25-16592S financed by GAČR. Miroslav Bulíček is a member of the Nečas Center for Mathematical Modeling. Jakub Woźnicki was supported by National Science Centre, Poland through project no. 2023/49/N/ST1/02737.
Miroslav Bulíček, Tomáš Los, Jakub Woźnicki

Abstract
Viscoelastic rate-type fluid models constitute a fundamental framework for the mathematical description of complex materials exhibiting coupled elastic and viscous effects, with a wide range of applications in engineering, biomaterials, and medicine. In realistic regimes, thermal effects are essential and lead to strongly coupled systems in which heat conduction and temperature-dependent constitutive laws play a decisive role. In this paper, we develop a thermodynamically consistent model for heat-conducting viscoelastic rate-type fluids. We establish the existence of a global weak solution in the full three-dimensional setting. In contrast to the existing literature, no smallness, regularity, or structural restrictions on the initial data are imposed beyond natural energy and entropy bounds, and no additional regularising mechanisms such as artificial stress diffusion are required. The analysis is based on a weak–strong framework combining energy and entropy balances with compactness tools, allowing us to treat the full nonlinear coupling between the fluid velocity, the temperature, and the elastic stress.



MSC
35K55, 35M13 , 35Q35 , 76A10

1
Introduction

The main goal of the paper is to establish a long-time, large-data existence result for a system of partial differential equations describing the flow of an incompressible viscoelastic rate-type heat-conducting fluid model in a bounded
d
-dimensional domain
\Omega\subset\mathbb{R}^{d}
over the time interval
(0,T)
with
T>0
. Denoting
Q_{T}:=(0,T)\times\Omega
, the system of equations we are interested in is the following system assumed to be satisfied in
Q_{T}
:

\displaystyle\diver\bm{v}

\displaystyle=0,

(1.1)

\displaystyle\rho\partial_{t}\bm{v}+\rho\diver(\bm{v}\otimes\bm{v})-\diver\mathbb{T}

\displaystyle=\rho\bm{f},

(1.2)

\displaystyle\partial_{t}\mathbb{B}+\mbox{Div}(\mathbb{B}\otimes\bm{v})-\nabla\bm{v}\mathbb{B}-\mathbb{B}(\nabla\bm{v})^{T}+\mathbb{H}

\displaystyle=\mathbb{O},

(1.3)

\displaystyle\rho\partial_{t}e+\rho\diver(e\bm{v})+\diver\bm{q}-\mathbb{T}:\mathbb{D}

\displaystyle=0.

(1.4)

Here,
\rho>0
is the constant density,
\bm{v}:Q_{T}\to\mathbb{R}^{d}
is the velocity field,
p:Q_{T}\to\mathbb{R}
denotes the pressure,
\mathbb{T}:Q_{T}\to\mathbb{R}^{d\times d}_{\textrm{sym}}
denotes the Cauchy stress tensor,
\bm{f}:Q_{T}\to\mathbb{R}^{d}
denotes the density of the external body forces,
\mathbb{B}:Q_{T}\to\mathbb{R}^{d\times d}_{\textrm{sym}}
describes the left Cauchy–Green tensor related to the natural configuration, and
\mathbb{H}:Q_{T}\to\mathbb{R}^{d\times d}_{\textrm{sym}}
is responsible for the dissipative effects in the extra stress tensor and relaxation processes, while
\bm{q}:Q_{T}\to\mathbb{R}^{d}
is the heat flux and
e:Q_{T}\to\mathbb{R}_{+}
denotes the internal energy of the fluid. Equation (
1.1
) is the incompressibility condition, the balance of linear momentum is described by (
1.2
), the evolution of
\mathbb{B}
is described by (
1.3
), which contains the upper convected Oldroyd derivative, and the balance of internal energy is given by (
1.4
). The system must be completed by the constitutive relations. Here, we assume that the primary variable is also the temperature
\theta:Q_{T}\to\mathbb{R}_{+}
, and the constitutive relations are of the form:

\displaystyle\bm{q}

\displaystyle=-\kappa(\theta)\nabla\theta,

(1.5)

\displaystyle\mathbb{H}

\displaystyle=\tau(\theta)\left(\mathbb{B}^{2}-\mathbb{B}\right)+\alpha(\theta)(\mathbb{B}-\mathbb{I}),

(1.6)

\displaystyle\mathbb{T}

\displaystyle=-p\mathbb{I}+2\nu(\theta)\mathbb{D}+2\rho g(\theta)\mathbb{B},

(1.7)

\displaystyle e

\displaystyle=c_{v}\theta+(g(\theta)-\theta g^{\prime}(\theta))(\mbox{tr }\mathbb{B}-d-\ln\det\mathbb{B}),

(1.8)

\displaystyle\mathbb{B}

\displaystyle=\mathbb{F}\mathbb{F}^{T},

(1.9)

\displaystyle\det\mathbb{F}

\displaystyle>0.

(1.10)

The heat flux is assumed to satisfy the classical Fourier law with the heat conductivity
\kappa(\theta)
, the tensorial quantity
\mathbb{H}
represents the combination of the Giesekus and Oldroyd models for dissipation with the relaxation parameters
\tau(\theta)
and
\alpha(\theta)
, and the Cauchy stress is given as the Newtonian part
\nu(\theta)\mathbb{D}
, represented by the viscosity
\nu(\theta)
and by the symmetric velocity gradient

\mathbb{D}:=\frac{\nabla\bm{v}+(\nabla\bm{v})^{T}}{2},

and the extra stress tensor
2\rho g(\theta)\mathbb{B}
. Moreover, we require the matrix
\mathbb{B}
to be positive definite, which is guaranteed by (
1.9
). Moreover, the symbol
\mathbb{O}
denotes the zero tensor in
\mathbb{R}^{d\times d}
. Finally, the internal energy
e
is given by (
1.8
).

The system of equations above must be completed by appropriate initial and boundary conditions. Hence,
for the boundary conditions, the paper deals with the following set of boundary conditions

\begin{split}\bm{v}\cdot\bm{n}=0,\quad(\mathbb{T}\bm{n})_{\tau}=-\gamma_{*}\bm{v}_{\tau},\qquad\nabla\theta\cdot\bm{n}=0\qquad\textrm{ on }(0,T)\times\partial\Omega.\end{split}

(1.11)

Here,
\mathbb{I}
is the identity tensor,
\bm{z}_{\tau}:=\bm{z}-(\bm{z}\cdot\bm{n})\bm{n}
stands for the tangential part of a vector
\bm{z}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
with respect to
\partial\Omega
, where
\bm{n}
denotes the unit outward normal vector.The above boundary conditions describe the so-called Navier slip boundary condition for the velocity field, where
\gamma_{*}\geq 0
is the slip parameter and the zero flux boundary condition for the internal energy.

To complete the system, we prescribe the following initial conditions for
\bm{v}
,
\mathbb{F}
, and
\theta

\begin{split}\bm{v}(0,\bm{\cdot})=\bm{v}_{0},\quad\mathbb{F}(0,\bm{\cdot})=\mathbb{F}_{0},\quad\theta(0,\bm{\cdot})=\theta_{0}\quad\textrm{ in }\Omega\end{split}

(1.12)

where
\bm{v}_{0}
,
\mathbb{F}_{0}
and
\theta_{0}
are given. Furthermore, the initial conditions for the internal energy and for the tensor
\mathbb{B}
are then given as

\displaystyle\mathbb{B}_{0}(0,\bm{\cdot}):=\mathbb{F}_{0}\mathbb{F}_{0}^{T},\quad e_{0}(0,\bm{\cdot}):=c_{v}\theta_{0}+(g(\theta_{0})-\theta_{0}g^{\prime}(\theta_{0}))(\mbox{tr }\mathbb{B}_{0}-d-\ln\det\mathbb{B}_{0})\qquad\textrm{ in }\Omega.

In the above model, we introduced several material parameters and functions. Our main goal is to establish the theory for a general large class of material parameters. Hence, we consider that
\rho,c_{v}\in(0,\infty)
and
\gamma_{*}\in[0,\infty)
are given constants and that
g,\nu,\tau,\kappa:\mathbb{R}{+}\to\mathbb{R}{+}
are given continuous functions satisfying for some
K\geq 1
.

\displaystyle K^{-1}

\displaystyle\leq\nu(\theta)\leq K,

(1.13)

\displaystyle K^{-1}

\displaystyle\leq\tau(\theta)\leq K,

(1.14)

\displaystyle K^{-1}

\displaystyle\leq\kappa(\theta)\leq K,

(1.15)

\displaystyle 0

\displaystyle\leq\alpha(\tau)\leq K,

(1.16)

\displaystyle 0

\displaystyle\leq g(\theta)\leq K.

(1.17)

In addition, we require
g\in C^{2}(\mathbb{R}_{+})
and that it satisfies

\displaystyle g^{\prime\prime}(\theta)

\displaystyle\leq 0\leq g^{\prime}(\theta)\qquad\textrm{ for all }\theta\in(0,\infty),

(1.18)

\displaystyle\limsup_{\theta\to\infty}\theta g^{\prime}(\theta)

\displaystyle=:L<\infty.

(1.19)

Our main result of the paper is formulated as follows.

Theorem
.

Let
\Omega\subset\mathbb{R}^{d}
be Lipschitz and let the initial conditions belong to the natural energy-entropy function space. Assume that the parameters satisfy the assumptions (
1.13
)–(
1.19
). Then there exists a weak dissipative global-in-time solution to the system (
1.1
)–(
1.12
).

Although the above theorem precisely describes the key result of the paper, it is formulated rather vaguely. Therefore, in the remainder of this introduction, we provide a detailed description of what we mean by a dissipative entropy solution and identify the correct function spaces for initial data. Furthermore, we give an indication of the thermodynamical background of the above system and also of the role of various boundary conditions. We also highlight the key novelty compared to the existing literature. The final results are then precisely and rigorously proven in Section
2
(see Theorem
2.5
) and Section
3
(see Theorem
3.9
). While in Section
2
we deal only with the sequential stability of the solution, Section
3
provides the complete existence proof.

1.1
Thermodynamical background and several notions of the solution

There is a long journey in building the physical background for the studied model, which goes back to
[
16
]
, who derived a model for describing viscoelastic and relaxation effects in fluids. Such an approach was then extended by the celebrated work of Oldroyd
[
37
]
, who put viscoelastic rate-type models on a rigorous theoretical background. Later, due to several inconsistencies between observations, Giesekus
[
25
]
proposed a new model with better properties, and this model is now frequently used. In fact, it is the model described by (
1.1
)–(
1.8
), with a constant temperature instead. There are also much more general classes of such fluids, see e.g.
[
28
]
. Furthermore, stress diffusion effects can be incorporated, which then simplify the analysis of the corresponding model. However, a reasonable thermodynamical background for such models, including temperature effects, was missing. The first theoretical results dealing with such models including temperature effects are due to
[
38
]
, where the complete thermodynamical background is carefully built and the essential quantities are identified, which are later also used in the analytical parts. The Rajagopal and Srinivasa approach led to a huge development not only at the level of modelling but also at the level of analytical results. We further refer the reader to
[
38
,
39
,
35
,
34
]
for references on particular applications.

Moreover, this approach, where the Helmholtz free energy plays a central role, was then adopted to develop a large cascade of possible models with full complexity; see, for example,
[
26
,
36
,
31
]
. We may also refer to
[
20
]
for the first systematic approach or to
[
27
]
, where the associated entropy estimates were derived for the first time.

To follow this idea, we introduce the Helmholtz free energy

\psi(\theta,\mathbb{B}):=-c_{v}\theta(\ln\theta-1)+g(\theta)\tilde{\psi}(\mathbb{B}),

(1.20)

where
\tilde{\psi}
is of the classical form
1
1

1

Note that it may have many different forms depending on the context. The only physically motivated requirement is that it has a minimum for
\mathbb{B}=\mathbb{I}
and it tends to infinity whenever
|\mathbb{B}|\to\infty
or
|\mathbb{B}^{-1}|\to\infty
.

\tilde{\psi}(\mathbb{B})=\mbox{tr }\mathbb{B}-d-\ln\det\mathbb{B}.

(1.21)

Then the general concept for dealing with other thermodynamical potentials is the following. We define the entropy
\eta
as

\eta(\theta,\mathbb{B}):=-\frac{\partial\psi(\theta,\mathbb{B})}{\partial\theta},

(1.22)

and the internal energy as

e(\theta,\mathbb{B}):=\psi(\theta,\mathbb{B})+\theta\eta(\theta,\mathbb{B}).

(1.23)

With our particular choice of the free energy (
1.21
), we see that
e
has the form (
1.8
). Moreover, it follows from the definition of
\tilde{\psi}
that
\tilde{\psi}(\mathbb{B})\geq 0
. Then, using (
1.8
), we see that
e
is a nondecreasing function of the temperature if and only if the function
g
is concave, see assumption (
1.18
). Furthermore, the entropy
\eta
is given by

\displaystyle\eta(\theta,\mathbb{B})

\displaystyle:=c_{v}\ln\theta-g^{\prime}(\theta)\tilde{\psi}(\mathbb{B}).

(1.24)

Next, we define the total energy as the sum of the kinetic and internal energies, i.e.

\displaystyle E(\theta,\mathbb{B},\bm{v})

\displaystyle:=\frac{|\bm{v}|^{2}}{2}+e(\theta,\mathbb{B}).

(1.25)

We now derive the equations for these two quantities, and our main goal is to replace (
1.4
) by something formally equivalent at the level of classical solutions. The reason for this consideration is that the sought solution will not be regular enough (or we do not know whether it exists) to give any meaning to (
1.4
). To derive the equation for
E
, it is enough to take the scalar product of (
1.2
) with
\bm{v}
, sum it with (
1.4
), and, using the identity (
1.1
), we obtain

\rho\partial_{t}E+\rho\diver\left((E+p)\bm{v}\right)-\diver\left(\kappa(\theta)\nabla\theta\right)=\diver\big((2\nu(\theta)\mathbb{D}+2\rho g(\theta)\mathbb{B})\bm{v}\big),

(1.26)

To derive the equation for
\eta
, we divide (
1.4
) by
\theta
to obtain

\begin{split}\frac{\rho}{\theta}\left(\partial_{t}e+\diver(e\bm{v})\right)+\diver\left(\frac{\bm{q}}{\theta}\right)=\frac{\mathbb{T}:\mathbb{D}}{\theta}-\frac{\bm{q}\cdot\nabla\theta}{\theta^{2}}=\frac{2\nu(\theta)|\mathbb{D}|^{2}+2\rho g(\theta)\mathbb{D}:\mathbb{B}}{\theta}+\frac{\kappa(\theta)|\nabla\theta|^{2}}{\theta^{2}},\end{split}

(1.27)

where we used the constitutive relations
(
1.7
) and (
1.5
). Then, we take the scalar product of (
1.3
) with
\partial_{\mathbb{B}}\psi(\theta,\mathbb{B})
to deduce

\left(\partial_{t}\mathbb{B}+\mbox{Div}(\mathbb{B}\otimes\bm{v})\right):\partial_{\mathbb{B}}\psi(\theta,\mathbb{B})-\left(\nabla\bm{v}\mathbb{B}+\mathbb{B}(\nabla\bm{v})^{T}\right):\partial_{\mathbb{B}}\psi(\theta,\mathbb{B})+\mathbb{H}:\partial_{\mathbb{B}}\psi(\theta,\mathbb{B})=\mathbb{O}.

(1.28)

Since
\diver\bm{v}=0
, we can rewrite the first term on the left-hand side with the help of the definitions of
\eta
and
e
in (
1.22
) and (
1.23
) as

\displaystyle\left(\partial_{t}\mathbb{B}+\mbox{Div}(\mathbb{B}\otimes\bm{v})\right):\partial_{\mathbb{B}}\psi(\theta,\mathbb{B})

\displaystyle=\left(\partial_{t}\psi(\theta,\mathbb{B})+\diver(\psi(\theta,\mathbb{B})\bm{v}\right)+\left(\partial_{t}\theta+\diver(\theta\bm{v})\right)\eta(\theta,\mathbb{B})

\displaystyle=\left(\partial_{t}(e(\theta,\mathbb{B})-\theta\eta(\theta,\mathbb{B}))+\diver((e(\theta,\mathbb{B})-\theta\eta(\theta,\mathbb{B}))\bm{v}\right)+\left(\partial_{t}\theta+\diver(\theta\bm{v})\right)\eta(\theta,\mathbb{B})

\displaystyle=\left(\partial_{t}e(\theta,\mathbb{B})+\diver(e(\theta,\mathbb{B})\bm{v})\right)-\theta\left(\partial_{t}\eta(\theta,\mathbb{B})+\diver(\eta(\theta,\mathbb{B})\bm{v})\right).

We also evaluate the second and the third terms on the left-hand side of (
1.28
) as follows. We note that it follows from (
1.20
) and (
1.21
) that
\partial_{\mathbb{B}}\psi(\theta,\mathbb{B})=g(\theta)(\mathbb{I}-\mathbb{B}^{-1})
, provided that
\mathbb{B}
is positive definite. Using this, the fact that
\diver\bm{v}=0
and the definition of
\mathbb{H}
in (
1.6
), we see that

\displaystyle\left(\mathbb{H}-\nabla\bm{v}\mathbb{B}-\mathbb{B}(\nabla\bm{v})^{T}\right):\partial_{\mathbb{B}}\psi(\theta,\mathbb{B})

\displaystyle=g(\theta)\left(\tau(\theta)\left(\mathbb{B}^{2}-\mathbb{B}\right)+\alpha(\theta)(\mathbb{B}-\mathbb{I})-\nabla\bm{v}\mathbb{B}-\mathbb{B}(\nabla\bm{v})^{T}\right):\left(\mathbb{I}-\mathbb{B}^{-1}\right)

\displaystyle=g(\theta)\left(\tau(\theta)|\mathbb{B}-\mathbb{I}|^{2}+\alpha(\theta)|\mathbb{B}^{\frac{1}{2}}-\mathbb{B}^{-\frac{1}{2}}|^{2}+2\mathbb{D}:\mathbb{B}\right).

Finally, we insert this relation into (
1.28
), multiply the result by
\rho/\theta
, and subtract it from (
1.27
) to deduce the entropy identity

\rho\partial_{t}\eta+\rho\diver(\eta\bm{v})-\diver\left(\frac{\kappa(\theta)\nabla\theta}{\theta}\right)=\frac{\kappa(\theta)|\nabla\theta|^{2}}{\theta^{2}}+\frac{2\nu(\theta)|\mathbb{D}|^{2}+\rho\tau(\theta)g(\theta)|\mathbb{B}-\mathbb{I}|^{2}+\rho\alpha(\theta)g(\theta)|\mathbb{B}^{\frac{1}{2}}-\mathbb{B}^{-\frac{1}{2}}|^{2}}{\theta}.

(1.29)

Hence, we formally derived two identities: one for the total energy (
1.26
) and the other for the entropy (
1.29
). Note that both are formally equivalent to (
1.4
). However, this represents a very fundamental change of perspective when dealing with these identities. The internal energy equation is, in fact, deduced from the equation for the total energy, which plays the role of the second law of thermodynamics. Therefore, (
1.26
) should be preferred, and one should focus on it and try to formulate it in a weak sense. However, contrary to the classical Navier–Stokes equations, in (
1.26
) a pressure appears that must be introduced as an integrable quantity. This is the key drawback, and we know that this might not be possible, for example, for Dirichlet boundary conditions. On the other hand, one can follow the ideas in
[
22
]
for the spatially periodic setting or
[
8
,
9
]
for the case of Navier’s slip boundary conditions.

Since pressure
p
might not exist for Dirichlet boundary conditions, we can go further and require only that the total energy satisfies the so-called energy inequality. That is, integrating (
1.26
) over
\Omega
, using integration by parts and the boundary conditions (
1.11
), we obtain (we can even replace the equality sign by an inequality)

\frac{d}{dt}\rho\int_{\Omega}E+\gamma_{*}\int_{\partial\Omega}|\bm{v}|^{2}\leq 0,

(1.30)

where no pressure appears. In fact, here we lose the formal equivalence with the internal energy balance. Therefore, we complete the system with (
1.29
), where we replace the equality sign by the inequality sign
\geq
, which is perfectly motivated by the second law of thermodynamics. Note that if we have a smooth solution to (
1.1
)–(
1.3
), which in addition satisfies (
1.30
) and (
1.29
) with the inequality sign “
\geq
", then it must also satisfy (
1.4
). Hence, we see that we have “deduced” a notion of a solution that is compatible with the classical solution if the latter exists. Such a solution will be called a dissipative weak solution. We want to emphasise that, in the context of heat-conducting fluids, such an approach was introduced by E. Feireisl, and we refer the interested reader to the classical books
[
21
,
24
]
.

This is our preferred choice for the definition of a solution. To simplify the subsequent discussion, we now set
\rho\equiv 1
and also
\alpha(\theta)\equiv 0
. Note that this does not change the analysis at all.

1.2
Reformulation of the rate-type model

The next difficulty we have to deal with arises in equation (
1.3
). Thanks to the energy estimates (derived later), we shall find that the tensorial quantities that are not under derivatives are only
L^{1}
-integrable, and therefore it may not be easy to identify the limit and to construct the solutions. Therefore, we introduce another quantity that will formally play the role of the square root of
\mathbb{B}
. Here, we closely follow the idea of Masmoudi
[
32
]
and introduce the following

\partial_{t}\mathbb{F}+\mathrm{Div}(\mathbb{F}\otimes\bm{v})-\nabla\bm{v}\mathbb{F}+\frac{\tau(\theta)}{2}(\mathbb{F}\mathbb{F}^{T}\mathbb{F}-\mathbb{F})=\mathbb{O},

(1.31)

Assume for a moment that
\bm{v}
,
\theta
, and
\mathbb{F}
are smooth and satisfy (
1.31
). Then, define

\mathbb{B}:=\mathbb{F}\mathbb{F}^{T},

and we show that
\mathbb{B}
satisfies (
1.3
). Indeed, multiplying (
1.31
) from the right by
\mathbb{F}^{T}
and using that
\diver\bm{v}=0
, we obtain

\partial_{t}\mathbb{F}\mathbb{F}^{T}+\bm{v}\cdot(\nabla\mathbb{F}\mathbb{F}^{T})-\nabla\bm{v}\mathbb{F}\mathbb{F}^{T}+\frac{\tau(\theta)}{2}(\mathbb{F}\mathbb{F}^{T}\mathbb{F}-\mathbb{F})\mathbb{F}^{T}=\mathbb{O}.

Next, we take the transpose of equation (
1.31
) and multiply the result from the left by
\mathbb{F}
, obtaining

\mathbb{F}\partial_{t}\mathbb{F}^{T}+\bm{v}\cdot(\mathbb{F}\nabla\mathbb{F}^{T})-\mathbb{F}\mathbb{F}^{T}(\nabla\bm{v})^{T}+\frac{\tau(\theta)}{2}\mathbb{F}(\mathbb{F}^{T}\mathbb{F}\mathbb{F}^{T}-\mathbb{F}^{T})=\mathbb{O}.

Hence, summing the above identities and using the fact that
\mathbb{F}\mathbb{F}^{T}=\mathbb{B}
, we deduce (
1.3
).

Note that the above formal procedure can be made rigorous by using the renormalisation technique developed by Di Perna and Lions, see
[
19
]
. Indeed, following their approach step by step, one can show that (
1.31
) implies (
1.3
), provided that
\mathbb{F}\in L^{4}(Q_{T})^{d\times d}
and
\bm{v}\in L^{2}(0,T;W^{1,2}(\Omega)^{d})
with
\diver\bm{v}=0
, which is exactly the setting of the present paper; see also
[
13
,
14
]
.

1.3
Novelty and bibliographical remarks

The system under consideration is undoubtedly one of the most studied in the context of viscoelastic fluid models, even in the constant-temperature setting, where it is already quite difficult. However, for the iconic model, the Oldroyd-B model, which corresponds to the setting with
\alpha(\theta)\equiv 1
and
\tau(\theta)\equiv 0
, there is no global-in-time large-data result. The only global-in-time existence result is known for a simplified model in the so-called co-rotational case, see
[
30
]
. Thus, the only available results for the Oldroyd-B model are obtained by adding an artificial stress diffusion term. For such models, one may establish a global-in-time theory, see e.g.
[
2
,
18
,
29
]
.

For the Giesekus model, the path towards a global-in-time theory was outlined in
[
33
]
, albeit without details. Thus, the first rigorous results for Giesekus models are due to
[
3
]
, where an additional stress diffusion term is added, while the original Giesekus model was finally treated in
[
13
,
14
]
; see also
[
10
]
for relevant compressible but very simplified models.

Finally, when switching to models with temperature dependence, not much is known. Indeed, starting with purely viscous fluids, one may follow the basic strategy outlined mainly by Feireisl (for compressible fluids) and Feireisl and Málek (for incompressible fluids) and deduce a relatively satisfactory theory. The interested reader is referred to the following articles
[
6
,
5
,
22
,
8
,
9
]
for relevant results, studies, and techniques used.

Finally, for problems involving both elasticity and temperature effects, the very first rigorous global-in-time large-data result is presented in
[
11
]
, where, however, only spherical stresses were assumed, i.e.
\mathbb{B}=b\mathbb{I}
. A significant improvement of this result towards more realistic models is developed in
[
4
]
, where the authors again consider a modification of the Giesekus model by adding stress diffusion into the system and treating only a very simplified form of the internal energy, namely the case when
g(\theta)=\theta
, which then leads to many reductions in the related system. A very relevant study for our problem is
[
15
]
, where the existence result is established for models in which the macroscopic equation for
\mathbb{B}
is replaced by its microscopic Fokker–Planck version. Finally, the only available result dealing with the Giesekus heat-conducting fluid is established in
[
7
]
, where, however, only the two-dimensional case is treated and where the authors assume rather non-realistic conditions on the heat conductivity; namely, assumption (
1.15
) is replaced by

K^{-1}(1+\theta)^{r}\leq\kappa(\theta)\leq K(1+\theta)^{r}

for some sufficiently large
r
. Hence, our main novelty here is that we are able to treat the full three-dimensional (in fact, any-dimensional) case, we do not require any additional mollification due to stress diffusion, and, most importantly, we do not need to impose any unrealistic growth assumptions on the material parameters.

1.4
Notation, structure of the paper, and auxiliary tools

To emphasize, we recall that throughout the whole paper we assume (and do not mention it anymore) that
\Omega\subset\mathbb{R}^{d}
is a bounded open domain with a Lipschitz boundary, with dimension
d\geq 2
,
T\in(0,\infty)
is the length of an arbitrary but fixed time interval, and
Q_{T}:=(0,T)\times\Omega
is the space–time cylinder.

Further, in the paper we use the standard notions for Lebesgue, Sobolev and Bochner spaces. In case we want to emphasize the vector- or tensor-valued character of a function, this is indicated by the exponent, e.g.
L^{p}(\Omega)^{d}
for vector-valued functions, and
L^{p}(\Omega)^{d\times d}
for tensor-valued functions. To denote zero trace, we use the abbreviation
W^{1,p}_{0}(\Omega)
for Sobolev spaces. Due to the special character of the requirement on the velocity field, we also define the function spaces

\displaystyle W_{\bm{n}}^{1,p}

\displaystyle:=\{\bm{v}\in W^{1,p}(\Omega)^{d}:\;\bm{v}\cdot\bm{n}=0\textrm{ on }\partial\Omega\},

\displaystyle W_{\bm{n},\diver}^{1,p}

\displaystyle:=\{\bm{v}\in W_{\bm{n}}^{1,p}:\;\diver\bm{v}=0\textrm{ in }\Omega\},

\displaystyle L^{2}_{\bm{n},\diver}

\displaystyle:=\overline{W_{\bm{n},\diver}^{1,2}}^{\|\,\|_{L^{2}}}

Next, for any Banach space
X
, we denote its dual by
X^{*}
. Furthermore, the space of Radon measures on a set
Z
is denoted by
\mathcal{M}(Z)
. For
F\in X^{*}
and
f\in X
, we use
\langle F,f\rangle_{X}
to denote duality pairing, and in the absence of ambiguity we suppress the use of the subscript
X
. Furthermore,
p^{\prime}:=p/(p-1)
denotes the dual exponent.

The structure of the rest of the paper is as follows. In Section
2
, we derive a priori estimates of hypothetical smooth solutions to (
1.1
)–(
1.12
), and prove their weak sequential stability. In Section
3
, we establish the existence of a solution. At the beginning of each section, we give the precise definition of each statement.

The rest of this introductory part is devoted to recalling several useful lemmas used in the paper. We start with the introduction of the rescaled entropy.

Proposition 1.1
.

If
\theta>0
and
\det\mathbb{B}>0
in
Q_{T}
, and
(\bm{v},p,\mathbb{B},\theta)
is a smooth solution to (
1.1
)–(
1.3
), then (
1.4
) is equivalent to (
1.29
) and also to

\displaystyle\rho\partial_{t}\eta_{\lambda}+\rho\diver(\eta_{\lambda}\bm{v})+\left(\rho\tau(\theta)|\mathbb{B}-\mathbb{I}|^{2}-2\rho\mathbb{B}:\mathbb{D}\right)(g^{\prime}(\theta)\theta^{\lambda}-h_{\lambda}(\theta))-\diver\left(\frac{\kappa(\theta)\nabla\theta}{\theta^{1-\lambda}}\right)

\displaystyle\hskip 18.49988pt=(1-\lambda)\frac{\kappa(\theta)|\nabla\theta|^{2}}{\theta^{2-\lambda}}+\frac{2\nu(\theta)|\mathbb{D}|^{2}+\rho\tau(\theta)g(\theta)|\mathbb{B}-\mathbb{I}|^{2}}{\theta^{1-\lambda}}

(1.32)

with

\displaystyle\eta_{\lambda}

\displaystyle:=c_{v}\frac{\theta^{\lambda}}{\lambda}-h_{\lambda}(\theta)\tilde{\psi}(\mathbb{B}),\hskip 9.24994pt\lambda\in(0,1),

(1.33)

where
h_{\lambda}(\theta)
denotes a primitive function to
\theta^{\lambda}g^{\prime\prime}(\theta)
.

Proof.

We have already proved the equivalence of (
1.4
) and (
1.29
). Therefore, we focus on the second equivalence, namely that (
1.4
) is equivalent to (
1.32
) for arbitrary
\lambda\in(0,1)
. Multiplying (
1.4
) by
\theta^{\lambda-1}
, using (
1.7
), (
1.8
) and (
1.1
), we obtain

\begin{split}&\frac{\rho c_{v}}{\lambda}(\partial_{t}\theta^{\lambda}+\nabla\theta^{\lambda}\cdot\bm{v})-\rho(\partial_{t}\theta+\nabla\theta\cdot\bm{v})h_{\lambda}^{\prime}(\theta)\tilde{\psi}(\mathbb{B})\\
&\quad+\rho\left(\partial_{t}\tilde{\psi}(\mathbb{B})+\nabla\tilde{\psi}(\mathbb{B})\cdot\bm{v}\right)(g(\theta)-\theta g^{\prime}(\theta))\theta^{\lambda-1}-\diver\left(\frac{\kappa(\theta)\nabla\theta}{\theta^{1-\lambda}}\right)\\
&\qquad=(1-\lambda)\frac{\kappa(\theta)|\nabla\theta|^{2}}{\theta^{2-\lambda}}+\frac{2\nu(\theta)|\mathbb{D}|^{2}+2\rho g(\theta)\mathbb{B}:\mathbb{D}}{\theta^{1-\lambda}},\end{split}

which can be rewritten as (use also (
1.1
))

\begin{split}&\frac{\rho c_{v}}{\lambda}(\partial_{t}\theta^{\lambda}+\nabla\theta^{\lambda}\cdot\bm{v})-\rho\left(\partial_{t}(h_{\lambda}(\theta)\tilde{\psi}(\mathbb{B}))+\nabla(h_{\lambda}(\theta)\tilde{\psi}(\mathbb{B}))\cdot\bm{v}\right)\\
&\ +\rho\left(\partial_{t}\tilde{\psi}(\mathbb{B})+\nabla\tilde{\psi}(\mathbb{B})\cdot\bm{v}\right)(h_{\lambda}(\theta)-g^{\prime}(\theta)\theta^{\lambda})+\rho(\partial_{t}\tilde{\psi}(\mathbb{B})+\nabla\tilde{\psi}(\mathbb{B})\cdot\bm{v})g(\theta)\theta^{\lambda-1}\\
&\ -2\rho g(\theta)\theta^{\lambda-1}(\mathbb{B}-\mathbb{I}):\mathbb{D}-\diver\left(\frac{\kappa(\theta)\nabla\theta}{\theta^{1-\lambda}}\right)=(1-\lambda)\frac{\kappa(\theta)|\nabla\theta|^{2}}{\theta^{2-\lambda}}+\frac{2\nu(\theta)|\mathbb{D}|^{2}}{\theta^{1-\lambda}}.\end{split}

At this point, we use (
1.31
) multiplied scalarly by
2\rho(h_{\lambda}(\theta)-g^{\prime}(\theta)\theta^{\lambda})(\mathbb{F}-\mathbb{F}^{-T})
, resp. by
2\rho g(\theta)\theta^{\lambda-1}(\mathbb{F}-\mathbb{F}^{-T})
, to treat the third, resp. the fourth and fifth term on the left-hand side, and (we use also the symmetry of
\mathbb{B}
and (
1.1
)) conclude (
1.32
). All the arrangements were equivalent, hence (
1.32
) holds if and only if (
1.4
) holds.
∎

We continue with recalling several convergence tools.

Proposition 1.2

(Chacon’s biting lemma, see
[
1
]
)
.

Let
Q\subset\mathbb{R}^{d+1}
be a measurable set. Assume that a sequence
\{f_{k}\}_{k\in\mathbb{N}}
satisfies

\sup_{k\in\mathbb{N}}\|f_{k}\|_{L^{1}(Q)}<\infty.

(1.34)

Then there exist:
(i)

f\in L^{1}(Q)
,
(ii)
a non-decreasing family of measurable sets
\{E_{j}\}_{j\in\mathbb{N}}
,
E_{j}\subset Q
for all
j\in\mathbb{N}
, satisfying
\lim_{j\to\infty}|Q\setminus E_{j}|=0
,
and
(iii)
a subsequence of
\{f_{k}\}_{k\in\mathbb{N}}
, which we do not relabel, such that, for all
j\in\mathbb{N}
, the following holds:

f_{k}\rightharpoonup f\quad\textrm{weakly in }L^{1}(E_{j}).

(1.35)

In what follows, whenever we write

f_{k}\rightharpoonup f\textrm{ in the biting sense in }L^{1}(Q),

this means that, for the sequence
\{f_{k}\}_{k\in\mathbb{N}}
satisfying (
1.34
), we apply Chacon’s biting lemma (Proposition
1.2
) and obtain the corresponding
f\in L^{1}(Q)
and a sequence of sets
\{E_{j}\}_{j\in\mathbb{N}}
with the properties described above (together with a suitable subsequence of
\{f_{k}\}_{k\in\mathbb{N}}
, not relabeled) such that (
1.35
) holds. Moreover, it follows from the characterization of
L^{1}
-weakly convergent sequences that
\{f_{k}\}_{k\in\mathbb{N}}
is uniformly equi-integrable on
E_{j}
for any
j\in\mathbb{N}
. This means that, for any
\varepsilon>0
and any
j\in\mathbb{N}
, there exists
\delta>0
such that, for all measurable sets
U\subset E_{j}
satisfying
|U|\leq\delta
, there holds

\sup_{k\in\mathbb{N}}\int_{U}|f_{k}|\leq\varepsilon.

(1.36)

To be precise, the uniform bound (
1.34
) implies, for a suitable subsequence of
\{f_{k}\}_{k\in\mathbb{N}}
(not relabeled), the validity of estimate (
1.36
), which, for a further (not relabeled) subsequence of
\{f_{k}\}_{k\in\mathbb{N}}
, implies the convergence result (
1.35
). On the other hand, the convergence result (
1.35
) implies the validity of (
1.36
).

The next convergence result is an application of Proposition
1.2
to a Stokes-like system.

Proposition 1.3
.

Let
\{\tilde{\bm{w}}_{k}\}_{k\in\mathbb{N}}
and
\{\tilde{\mathbb{G}}_{k}\}_{k\in\mathbb{N}}
be sequences satisfying

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} \tilde{\bm{w}}_{k}&\rightharpoonup\tilde{\bm{w}}&&\textrm{weakly in }L^{2}(0,T;W_{\bm{n},\diver}^{1,2}),\\
\partial_{t}\tilde{\bm{w}}_{k}&\rightharpoonup\partial_{t}\tilde{\bm{w}}&&\textrm{weakly in }L^{2}(0,T;(W_{\bm{n},\diver}^{1,2})^{*}),\\
\tilde{\mathbb{G}}_{k}&\rightharpoonup\tilde{\mathbb{G}}&&\textrm{weakly in }(L^{2}(Q_{T}))^{d\times d}\end{aligned}

(1.37)

and fulfilling, for a.a.
t\in(0,T)
and all
\varphi\in W_{\bm{0},\diver}^{1,2}
, the following identity

\langle\partial_{t}\tilde{\bm{w}}_{k},\bm{\varphi}\rangle+\int_{\Omega}\tilde{\mathbb{G}}_{k}:\nabla\bm{\varphi}=0.

(1.38)

Then there holds

\displaystyle\tilde{\mathbb{G}}_{k}:\nabla\tilde{\bm{w}}_{k}\rightharpoonup\tilde{\mathbb{G}}:\nabla\tilde{\bm{w}}\qquad\textrm{in the biting sense in }L^{1}(Q_{T}).

(1.39)

Proof.

See
[
14
]
, Theorem 1.7 and Remark at the end of Section 2.
∎

Finally, the tool that will play the crucial role in the identification of the weak limit is the following.

Proposition 1.4
.

(Div-curl lemma,
[
17
]
)

Let
O\subset\mathbb{R}^{n}
be an open and bounded domain with a Lipschitz boundary and let
p,q\in(1+\infty)
with
\frac{1}{p}+\frac{1}{q}=1
. Suppose that
\{\bm{u}_{k}\}_{k\in\mathbb{N}}\subset(L^{p}(O))^{n}
,
\{\bm{v}_{k}\}_{k\in\mathbb{N}}\subset(L^{q}(O))^{n}
are sequences satisfying

\bm{u}_{k}\rightharpoonup\bm{u}\text{ weakly in }(L^{p}(O))^{n},\quad\bm{v}_{k}\rightharpoonup\bm{v}\text{ weakly in }(L^{q}(O))^{n},

and

\{\bm{u}_{k}\cdot\bm{v}_{k}\}_{k\in\mathbb{N}}\text{ is uniformly equiintegrable in }Q_{T}.

Finally, assume that

\displaystyle\diver\bm{u}_{k}

\displaystyle\rightarrow\diver\bm{u}\text{ strongly in }(W^{1,\infty}_{0}(O))^{*},

\displaystyle\mathrm{curl}\,\bm{v}_{k}

\displaystyle\rightarrow\mathrm{curl}\,\bm{v}\text{ strongly in }((W^{1,\infty}_{0}(O))^{n\choose 2})^{*}.

Then,

\bm{u}_{k}\cdot\bm{v}_{k}\rightharpoonup\bm{u}\cdot\bm{v}\text{ weakly in }L^{1}(O).

To conclude this part, it is essential to mention that the paper relies not only on the results mentioned above, but also on the following two classical techniques. First, we mention the theory of renormalisation for the transport equation developed in
[
19
]
, which is used when dealing with the equation for
\mathbb{B}
. Second, the compensated compactness method for heat-conducting fluids, introduced by Feireisl, see
[
21
]
, and further generalised and extended to other settings, see
[
24
,
23
]
and
[
12
]
. These methods are used to deduce compactness of the temperature
\theta
and the tensor
\mathbb{B}
.

2
A priori estimates and weak sequential stability

This section is devoted to the derivation of a priori estimates and to the proof of weak sequential stability. To simplify the notation and the presentation, we provide only the three-dimensional version of the theorem, but the corresponding result holds in any dimension with only minor modifications in the proof.

Theorem 2.5
.

Let the material parameters satisfy (
1.13
)–(
1.19
). Assume that for every
k\in\mathbb{N}
the smooth functions
\{\bm{v}_{k},p_{k},\theta_{k},\mathbb{F}_{k}\}
solve (
1.1
)–(
1.11
) and (
1.31
). Assume that
\det\mathbb{F}_{k}>0
in
Q_{T}
. Finally, assume that

\displaystyle\bm{v}_{k}(0)

\displaystyle\to\bm{v}_{0}

\displaystyle\textrm{strongly in }L^{2}_{\bm{n},\diver},

(2.1)

\displaystyle\mathbb{F}_{k}(0)

\displaystyle\to\mathbb{F}_{0}

\displaystyle\textrm{strongly in }L^{2}(\Omega)^{d\times d},

\displaystyle\theta_{k}(0)

\displaystyle\to\theta_{0}

\displaystyle\textrm{strongly in }L^{1}(\Omega),

\displaystyle e(\theta_{k}(0),\mathbb{B}_{k}(0))

\displaystyle\to e(\theta_{0},\mathbb{B}_{0})

\displaystyle\textrm{strongly in }L^{1}(\Omega),

\displaystyle\eta(\theta_{k}(0),\mathbb{B}_{k}(0))

\displaystyle\to\eta(\theta_{0},\mathbb{B}_{0})

\displaystyle\textrm{strongly in }L^{1}(\Omega).

Then there exists a subsequence (not relabeled) such that

\displaystyle\bm{v}_{k}

\displaystyle\rightharpoonup^{*}\bm{v}

\displaystyle\mbox{weakly-* in }L^{\infty}(0,T;L^{2}_{\bm{n},\diver})\cap L^{2}(0,T;W_{\bm{n},\diver}^{1,2}),

(2.2)

\displaystyle\mathbb{F}_{k}

\displaystyle\rightharpoonup^{*}\mathbb{F}

\displaystyle\mbox{weakly-* in }L^{\infty}(0,T;(L^{2}(\Omega))^{3\times 3})\cap L^{4}(Q_{T})^{3\times 3},

(2.3)

\displaystyle\theta_{k}

\displaystyle\rightharpoonup^{*}\theta

\displaystyle\mbox{weakly-* in }L^{\infty}(0,T;L^{1}(\Omega))\cap L^{\frac{5}{4}}(0,T;W^{1,\frac{5}{4}}(\Omega))\cap L^{\frac{5}{3}}(Q_{T}),

(2.4)

\displaystyle\partial_{t}\bm{v}_{k}

\displaystyle\rightharpoonup\partial_{t}\bm{v}

\displaystyle\mbox{weakly in }L^{\frac{4}{3}}(0,T;(W_{\bm{n},\diver}^{1,2})^{*}),

(2.5)

\displaystyle\partial_{t}\mathbb{F}_{k}

\displaystyle\rightharpoonup\partial_{t}\mathbb{F}

\displaystyle\mbox{weakly in }L^{\frac{4}{3}}(0,T;((W^{1,2}(\Omega))^{3\times 3})^{*}).

(2.6)

Moreover, for all
\bm{w}\in L^{\infty}(0,T;W^{1,\infty}_{\bm{n},\diver})
and all
\mathbb{A}\in L^{4}(Q_{T})^{3\times 3}
, the following identities hold:

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{T}\langle\partial_{t}\bm{v},\bm{w}\rangle

\displaystyle=\int_{Q_{T}}(\bm{v}\otimes\bm{v}):\nabla\bm{w}-\int_{Q_{T}}(2\nu(\theta)\mathbb{D}+g(\theta)\mathbb{F}\mathbb{F}^{T}):\nabla\bm{w}-\tau_{*}\int_{\Sigma_{T}}\bm{v}_{\tau}\cdot\bm{w}_{\tau},

(2.7)

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{T}\langle\partial_{t}\mathbb{F},\mathbb{A}\rangle

\displaystyle=\int_{Q_{T}}(\mathbb{F}\otimes\bm{v}):\nabla\mathbb{A}+\int_{Q_{T}}\left(\nabla\bm{v}\mathbb{F}-\frac{\tau(\theta)}{2}(\mathbb{F}\mathbb{F}^{T}\mathbb{F}-\mathbb{F})\right):\mathbb{A},

(2.8)

Finally, the entropy inequality and the total energy inequality are satisfied in the following sense: for all nonnegative
\varphi\in\mathcal{C}^{1}_{0}([0,T);\mathcal{C}^{1}(\Omega))
and all nonnegative
\xi\in\mathcal{C}^{1}_{0}([0,T))
, we have

\displaystyle\int_{Q_{T}}\eta(\theta,\mathbb{B})\partial_{t}\varphi

\displaystyle\geq\int_{\Omega}\eta(\theta_{0},\mathbb{B}_{0})\varphi(0)+\int_{Q_{T}}\eta\bm{v}\cdot\nabla\varphi-\int_{Q_{T}}\frac{\kappa(\theta)\nabla\theta}{\theta}\cdot\nabla\varphi

(2.9)

\displaystyle+\int_{Q_{T}}\varphi\left(\frac{\kappa(\theta)|\nabla\theta|^{2}}{\theta^{2}}+\frac{2\nu(\theta)|\mathbb{D}|^{2}+\rho\tau(\theta)g(\theta)|\mathbb{B}-\mathbb{I}|^{2}}{\theta}\right).

and

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{T}\xi^{\prime}\left(\int_{\Omega}\frac{|\bm{v}|^{2}}{2}+e(\theta,\mathbb{B})\right)+\int_{0}^{T}\xi\left(\gamma_{*}\int_{\partial\Omega}|\bm{v}|^{2}\right)\leq\xi(0)\int_{\Omega}\frac{|\bm{v}_{0}|^{2}}{2}+e(\theta_{0},\mathbb{B}_{0}).

(2.10)

The initial conditions are attained in the following sense:

\lim_{t\to 0_{+}}\left(\|\bm{v}(t)-\bm{v}_{0}\|_{2}+\|\theta(t)-\theta_{0}\|_{1}+\|\mathbb{F}(t)-\mathbb{F}_{0}\|_{2}\right)=0.

(2.11)

2.1
A priori estimates

Before we establish the a priori estimates for the problem (
1.1
)–(
1.12
), resp. for (
1.1
)–(
1.2
), (
1.31
), (
1.4
)–(
1.12
), we define an appropriate primitive function to
\theta^{\lambda}g^{\prime\prime}(\theta)
, i.e. the function
h_{\lambda}(\theta)
acting in the equation (
1.33
), and show, under certain growth assumptions on the function
g
, its properties, which will be used in the derivation of the a priori estimates.

Lemma 2.6
.

Let
g\in C^{2}(\mathbb{R})
be a nondecreasing concave function satisfying, for some
\delta\in(0,1)
, the condition

\displaystyle\lim_{\theta\to\infty}\theta^{1+\delta}g^{\prime}(\theta)

\displaystyle=:L_{\delta}\in[0,\infty).

(2.12)

Let the function
h_{\lambda}=h_{\lambda}(\theta)
, for
\lambda\in(0,1)
and
\theta\in(0,\infty)
, be defined as

h_{\lambda}(\theta):=\int_{\theta}^{\infty}-z^{\lambda}g^{\prime\prime}(z)\mathrm{d}z.

(2.13)

Then
h_{\lambda}(\theta)
is always nonnegative, bounded, and there holds

h_{\lambda}^{\prime}(\theta)=\theta^{\lambda}g^{\prime\prime}(\theta)\qquad\mbox{for all }\theta\in(0,\infty).

(2.14)

In addition, there exists a finite positive
C=C(g)
such that for any
\theta\in(0,\infty)

\begin{split}h_{\lambda}(\theta)\leq\theta^{\lambda}g^{\prime}(\theta)+\frac{\lambda}{(1-\lambda)}C(g)\theta^{\lambda-\delta-1}.\end{split}

(2.15)

Proof.

The nonnegativity of
h_{\lambda}(\theta)
for any
\theta\in(0,\infty)
follows immediately from the concavity of
g
(even the integrand in (
2.13
) is nonnegative).

To prove (
2.14
) it suffices to show that the integral in (
2.13
) is convergent for any fixed
\theta\in(0,\infty)
. Employing the integration by parts and (
1.19
) (together with the property
g^{\prime}\in C([0,\infty))
), we have, for any
\theta\in(0,\infty)
and some finite positive
C=C(g)
, that

\begin{split}h_{\lambda}(\theta)&=[-z^{\lambda}g^{\prime}(z)]_{\theta}^{\infty}+\int_{\theta}^{\infty}\lambda z^{\lambda-1}g^{\prime}(z)\mathrm{d}z\\
&=\theta^{\lambda}g^{\prime}(\theta)+\lambda\int_{\theta}^{\infty}z^{1+\delta}g^{\prime}(z)z^{\lambda-\delta-2}\mathrm{d}z\\
&\leq\theta^{\lambda}g^{\prime}(\theta)+{\lambda C(g)}\frac{\theta^{\lambda-\delta-1}}{(1+\delta-\lambda)},\end{split}

from which it follows that the integral in (
2.13
) is convergent, thus (
2.14
) holds. Moreover, we have proved the estimate (
2.15
).

The boundedness of the function
h_{\lambda}
is obvious. Indeed, for any
\theta\in(0,\infty)

0\leq h_{\lambda}(\theta)\leq\int_{0}^{\infty}-z^{\lambda}g^{\prime\prime}(z)\mathrm{d}z

(both inequalities following from the concavity of
g
), which is a convergent integral since the integral in (
2.13
) is convergent,
\lambda\in(0,1)
and
g^{\prime\prime}\in C([0,\infty))
.

∎

In the following, the (finite positive) constants of uniform bounds, whose exact values are not essential for our aims, are denoted as
C,\tilde{C},\hat{C},\overline{C}
,
C_{i}
,
i\in\mathbb{N}
,
K
,
\tilde{K}
,
\hat{K}
,
\overline{K}
,
\varepsilon
,
\tilde{\varepsilon}
. Their values can change throughout the text.

To simplify the presentation, we consider the three-dimensional setting, i.e. the flow domain
\Omega\subset\mathbb{R}^{3}
.

Proposition 2.7
.

Let
(\bm{v},p,\mathbb{F},\mathbb{B},e,\theta)
be a smooth solution to the problem (
1.1
)–(
1.2
), (
1.31
), (
1.4
)–(
1.12
). Let for simplicity
\rho=c_{v}=\gamma_{*}=1
,
\bm{f}\equiv\bm{0}
,
\theta>0
and
\det\mathbb{B}>0
in
Q_{T}
and let there exist
K\in(0,\infty)
such that

(
1.13
)–(
1.15
)

Then the following uniform estimates hold (
\tilde{\delta}\in(0,\frac{1}{4}]
is arbitrary):

\displaystyle\sup_{t\in(0,T)}(\|\bm{v}(t)\|_{2}^{2}+\|\theta(t)\|_{1}+\|e(t)\|_{1})

\displaystyle\leq C(\|\bm{v}_{0}\|_{2}^{2}+\|e_{0}\|_{1}),

(2.16)

\displaystyle\sup_{t\in(0,T)}\|\mathbb{F}(t)\|_{2}^{2}+\|\nabla\bm{v}\|_{2,Q_{T}}+\|\mathbb{F}\|_{4,Q_{T}}

\displaystyle\leq\tilde{C}(\delta,T,\Omega,g,\bm{v}_{0},\mathbb{B}_{0},\theta_{0}),

(2.17)

\displaystyle\|\theta\|_{\frac{5}{3}-\tilde{\delta},Q_{T}}+\|\nabla\theta\|_{\frac{5}{4}-\tilde{\delta},Q_{T}}

\displaystyle\leq\hat{C}(\delta,\tilde{\delta},T,\Omega,g,\bm{v}_{0},\mathbb{B}_{0},\theta_{0}),

(2.18)

\displaystyle\sup_{t\in(0,T)}(\|\ln\theta(t)\|_{1}+\|\ln\det\mathbb{B}(t)\|_{2})+\|\nabla\ln\theta\|_{2,Q_{T}}

\displaystyle\leq\overline{C}(\delta,T,\Omega,g,\bm{v}_{0},\mathbb{B}_{0},\theta_{0}).

(2.19)

Proof.

The estimate of
\|\bm{v}(t)\|_{2}
and
\|e(t)\|_{1}
by the right-hand side of (
2.16
) can be achieved, supposed that
e\geq 0
in
Q_{T}
, by summing (
1.2
) multiplied scalarly by
\bm{v}
with (
1.4
), integrating the result over
(0,t)\times\Omega
, where
t\in(0,T)
is arbitrary, and using (
1.1
), (
1.11
), (
1.12
), the integration by parts and the symmetry of
\mathbb{T}
(following from (
1.7
) and (
1.9
)). To obtain the validity of (
2.16
) it remains to prove that also
\|\theta(t)\|_{1}
is estimated by its right-hand side and that
e\geq 0
in
Q_{T}
. Since
g\in C^{2}(\mathbb{R})
is concave (which implies that
g(\theta)-\theta g^{\prime}(\theta)
is nondecreasing) and
g(0)\geq 0
, we have that

g(\theta)-\theta g^{\prime}(\theta)\geq g(0)\geq 0\quad\mbox{in }Q_{T}.

(2.20)

Moreover, since for

we have

\tilde{\psi}^{\prime}(\mathbb{B})=\mathbb{I}-\mathbb{B}^{-1},\quad\tilde{\psi}^{\prime\prime}(\mathbb{B})=\mathbb{B}^{-1}\otimes\mathbb{B}^{-1},

(2.21)

we see that
\tilde{\psi}
is convex and achieves its minimum at
\mathbb{B}=\mathbb{I}
. This minimum is equal to zero, hence

\tilde{\psi}(\mathbb{B})\geq 0\quad\mbox{in }Q_{T}.

(2.22)

As we assume that
\theta>0
, we get, by (
1.8
), (
2.20
) and (
2.22
), that
e>0
in
Q_{T}
and that the term
\|\theta(t)\|_{1}
is also estimated by the right-hand side of (
2.16
). Thus (
2.16
) holds.

The proof of (
2.17
) is split into the following three paragraphs. We may use Proposition
1.1
and Lemma
2.6
since all their assumptions are satisfied. In the first paragraph, we show that, for any
M\in(0,\infty)
, there holds

\int_{\{\theta\leq M\}}(|\mathbb{D}|^{2}+|\mathbb{B}-\mathbb{I}|^{2})\leq C(M,g,\bm{v}_{0},\mathbb{B}_{0},\theta_{0}).

(2.23)

Integrating (
1.29
), with
\eta
defined in (
1.24
), by parts over
\Omega
and using (
1.11
), we obtain (
\kappa
,
\nu
and
\tau
depend on
\theta
and
\mathbb{B}
)

\int_{\Omega}\partial_{t}(\ln\theta-g^{\prime}(\theta)\tilde{\psi}(\mathbb{B}))=\int_{\Omega}\left(\frac{\kappa|\nabla\theta|^{2}}{\theta^{2}}+\frac{2\nu|\mathbb{D}|^{2}+\tau g(\theta)|\mathbb{B}-\mathbb{I}|^{2}}{\theta}\right).

(2.24)

Integrating (
2.24
) over time and using the nonnegativity of
g^{\prime}(\theta)\tilde{\psi}(\mathbb{B})
, the property
\ln y\leq y
valid for all
y\in\mathbb{R}
, the estimate (
2.16
) and the conditions (
1.12
), we see that (here,
\tilde{C}
depends on
\mathbb{B}_{0}
and
\theta_{0}
, and
\hat{C}
depends on
\bm{v}_{0}
,
\mathbb{B}_{0}
and
\theta_{0}
)

\int_{Q_{T}}\frac{2\nu|\mathbb{D}|^{2}+\tau g(\theta)|\mathbb{B}-\mathbb{I}|^{2}}{\theta}\leq\sup_{t\in(0,T)}\int_{\Omega}\ln\theta(t)+\tilde{C}\leq\sup_{t\in(0,T)}\|\theta(t)\|_{1}+\tilde{C}\leq\hat{C}.

(2.25)

From (
1.13
), (
1.14
), (
2.25
) and the properties of
g
2
2

2

Since
g\in C^{2}([0,\infty))
is non constant, non decreasing and concave, we have that
g^{\prime}>0
in some right neighbourhood of
0
, hence, using also the nonnegativity of
g
, we have that
g(s)>0
for any
s>0
, and if
g(\theta)\to 0
as
\theta\to 0+
, then the l’Hospital rule gives

\lim_{\theta\to 0+}(g(\theta)/\theta)=g^{\prime}(0)>0.

we conclude the validity of (
2.23
).

Now, we improve the estimate (
2.23
) and show, for any
\lambda\in(0,1)
, that

\int_{Q_{T}}\frac{|\mathbb{D}|^{2}}{\theta^{1-\lambda}}\leq C(\delta,\lambda,T,\Omega,g,\bm{v}_{0},\mathbb{B}_{0},\theta_{0}),

(2.26)

where
\delta\in(0,1)
satisfies (
1.19
).
Integrating (
1.32
), with
\lambda\in(0,1)
arbitrary,
\eta_{\lambda}
defined in (
1.33
) and
h_{\lambda}(\theta)
defined in (
2.13
), over
\Omega
, and using the integration by parts and (
1.11
), we get

\begin{split}&\int_{\Omega}\partial_{t}\left(\frac{\theta^{\lambda}}{\lambda}-h_{\lambda}(\theta)\tilde{\psi}(\mathbb{B})\right)+\int_{\Omega}\left(\tau|\mathbb{B}-\mathbb{I}|^{2}-2\mathbb{B}:\mathbb{D}\right)(g^{\prime}(\theta)\theta^{\lambda}-h_{\lambda}(\theta))\\
&\quad=(1-\lambda)\int_{\Omega}\frac{\kappa|\nabla\theta|^{2}}{\theta^{2-\lambda}}+\int_{\Omega}\frac{2\nu|\mathbb{D}|^{2}+\tau g(\theta)|\mathbb{B}-\mathbb{I}|^{2}}{\theta^{1-\lambda}}.\end{split}

(2.27)

Integrating (
2.27
) over time, using also (
1.13
), (
1.14
), (
1.12
), the nonnegativity of
\tilde{\psi}(\mathbb{B})
,
h_{\lambda}(\theta)
and
g^{\prime}(\theta)
(see (
2.22
) and (
2.13
) with
g
being a concave nondecreasing function) and the Young inequality, we get

\begin{split}\int_{0}^{t}\int_{\Omega}\frac{|\mathbb{D}|^{2}+g(\theta)|\mathbb{B}-\mathbb{I}|^{2}}{\theta^{1-\lambda}}\leq\tilde{K}\int_{0}^{t}\int_{\Omega}(|\mathbb{B}-\mathbb{I}|^{2}+|\mathbb{D}|^{2})(g^{\prime}(\theta)\theta^{\lambda}+h_{\lambda}(\theta))\\
+\frac{\tilde{K}}{\lambda}\int_{\Omega}\theta^{\lambda}(t)+\tilde{C}(\mathbb{B}_{0},\theta_{0}).\end{split}

(2.28)

Let us note that
t\in(0,T)
in (
2.28
) is arbitrary and all integrands in (
2.28
) are nonnegative, hence, applying the fact that the nondecreasing
g
satisfies
g(s)>0
for any
s>0
(see the footnote on the previous page), the boundedness of
g^{\prime}(\theta)\theta^{\lambda}
and
h_{\lambda}(\theta)
(see Lemma
2.6
), the estimate (
2.16
) and the Hölder inequality, we can write for any
M\in(0,\infty)

\begin{split}&\int_{Q_{T}}\frac{|\mathbb{D}|^{2}}{\theta^{1-\lambda}}+\int_{\{\theta\geq M\}}\frac{|\mathbb{B}-\mathbb{I}|^{2}}{\theta^{1-\lambda}}\leq\left(1+\frac{1}{g(M)}\right)\left(\hat{K}(g)\int_{\{\theta\leq M\}}(|\mathbb{B}-\mathbb{I}|^{2}+|\mathbb{D}|^{2})\right.\\
&\left.\hskip 18.49988pt+\tilde{K}\int_{\{\theta\geq M\}}(|\mathbb{B}-\mathbb{I}|^{2}+|\mathbb{D}|^{2})(g^{\prime}(\theta)\theta^{\lambda}+h_{\lambda}(\theta))+\hat{C}(\lambda,T,\Omega,\bm{v}_{0},\mathbb{B}_{0},\theta_{0})\right).\end{split}

(2.29)

The first integral on the right-hand side of (
2.29
) is estimated by (
2.23
). The second integral on the right-hand side of (
2.29
) can be absorped by its left-hand side. Indeed, using (
1.19
) (and the fact
g^{\prime}\in C([0,\infty))
), we have

\begin{split}\int_{\{\theta\geq M\}}(|\mathbb{B}-\mathbb{I}|^{2}+|\mathbb{D}|^{2})g^{\prime}(\theta)\theta^{\lambda}\leq\int_{\{\theta\geq M\}}\frac{(|\mathbb{B}-\mathbb{I}|^{2}+|\mathbb{D}|^{2})}{\theta^{1-\lambda}M^{\delta}}g^{\prime}(\theta)\theta^{1+\delta}\\
\leq\frac{\overline{C}(g)}{M^{\delta}}\int_{\{\theta\geq M\}}\frac{|\mathbb{B}-\mathbb{I}|^{2}+|\mathbb{D}|^{2}}{\theta^{1-\lambda}},\end{split}

(2.30)

and, thanks to (
2.15
) and (
2.30
), it holds

\int_{\{\theta\geq M\}}(|\mathbb{B}-\mathbb{I}|^{2}+|\mathbb{D}|^{2})h_{\lambda}(\theta)\leq\frac{C_{1}(\lambda,g)}{M^{\delta}}\int_{\{\theta\geq M\}}\frac{|\mathbb{B}-\mathbb{I}|^{2}+|\mathbb{D}|^{2}}{\theta^{1-\lambda}}.

(2.31)

Hence, if
M\in(0,\infty)
is sufficiently large, we get from (
2.23
) and (
2.29
)–(
2.31
) that

\int_{\{\theta\geq M\}}\frac{(|\mathbb{D}|^{2}+|\mathbb{B}-\mathbb{I}|^{2})}{\theta^{1-\lambda}}\leq C_{2}(\delta,\lambda,M,T,\Omega,g,\bm{v}_{0},\mathbb{B}_{0},\theta_{0}),

(2.32)

which, again together with (
2.23
) and (
2.29
)–(
2.31
), gives the desired (
2.26
).

Now, we are ready to prove (
2.17
). Since the non constant function
g
is nonnegative, nondecreasing and bounded, there exists

\lim_{\theta\to\infty}g(\theta)=:g_{\infty}\in(0,\infty).

Multiplying (scalarly) (
1.2
) by
\bm{v}
and (
1.31
) by
2g_{\infty}\mathbb{F}
, summing the results, integrating by parts over time and space and using (
1.11
), (
1.12
) and the symmetry of
\mathbb{D}
and
\mathbb{F}\mathbb{F}^{T}
, we obtain for all
t\in(0,T)

\begin{split}\frac{\|\bm{v}(t)\|_{2}^{2}}{2}+g_{\infty}\|\mathbb{F}(t)\|_{2}^{2}+2\int_{0}^{t}\int_{\Omega}\nu|\mathbb{D}|^{2}+2\int_{0}^{t}\int_{\Omega}(g(\theta)-g_{\infty})\mathbb{F}\mathbb{F}^{T}:\mathbb{D}\\
+\int_{0}^{t}\int_{\partial\Omega}|\bm{v}_{\tau}|^{2}+g_{\infty}\int_{0}^{t}\int_{\Omega}\tau(|\mathbb{F}\mathbb{F}^{T}|^{2}-|\mathbb{F}|^{2})=\frac{\|\bm{v}_{0}\|_{2}^{2}}{2}+g_{\infty}\|\mathbb{F}_{0}\|_{2}^{2}.\end{split}

(2.33)

Applying the Young inequality on the last term in the first line, we get for any
\varepsilon\in(0,\infty)
and certain
\tilde{K}=\tilde{K}(\varepsilon)\in(0,\infty)

(g(\theta)-g_{\infty})\mathbb{F}\mathbb{F}^{T}:\mathbb{D}\leq\varepsilon|g(\theta)-g_{\infty}||\mathbb{F}\mathbb{F}^{T}|^{2}+\tilde{K}(\varepsilon)|g(\theta)-g_{\infty}||\mathbb{D}|^{2}.

(2.34)

Again, by the means of the Young inequality applied on the term
\tau|\mathbb{F}|^{2}
, we deduce from (
2.33
), (
2.34
) and the fact
\mathbb{B}_{0}=\mathbb{F}_{0}\mathbb{F}_{0}^{T}
(and (
1.13
), (
1.14
), the Korn inequality and the inequality
|\mathbb{F}|^{4}\leq 3|\mathbb{F}\mathbb{F}^{T}|^{2}
) that, to prove (
2.17
), it suffices to show that

\int_{Q_{T}}|g(\theta)-g_{\infty}||\mathbb{D}|^{2}\leq\tilde{C}(\delta,T,\Omega,g,\bm{v}_{0},\mathbb{B}_{0},\theta_{0}),

(2.35)

where
\delta\in(0,1)
satisfies (
1.19
).
However, taking into account (
2.26
) and the fact that
g
is nondecreasing and bounded, we see that (
2.35
) holds if for some
\overline{K}(\delta)\in(0,\infty)

and
L_{\delta}
defined in (
1.19
)

\lim_{\theta\to\infty}\theta^{\delta}(g_{\infty}-g(\theta))=\overline{K}(\delta)L_{\delta}\in[0,\infty).

The latter holds due to the l’Hospital rule and (
1.19
). Indeed,

\lim_{\theta\to\infty}\theta^{\delta}(g_{\infty}-g(\theta))=\lim_{\theta\to\infty}\frac{g_{\infty}-g(\theta)}{1/\theta^{\delta}}=\lim_{\theta\to\infty}\left(\frac{-g^{\prime}(\theta)}{-\delta/\theta^{1+\delta}}\right)=\frac{L_{\delta}}{\delta}.

The proof of (
2.17
) is finished.

Next, we prove the estimate (
2.18
).
Employing the boundedness of
g^{\prime}(\theta)\theta^{\lambda}
and
h_{\lambda}(\theta)
, the nonnegativity of
h_{\lambda}(\theta)\tilde{\psi}(\mathbb{B})
and the estimate (
2.17
) together with (
1.9
) and (
1.12
), we see that (
2.27
) integrated over time yields for all
t\in(0,T)
and
\lambda\in(0,1)

(1-\lambda)\int_{0}^{t}\int_{\Omega}\frac{\kappa|\nabla\theta|^{2}}{\theta^{2-\lambda}}\leq\frac{1}{\lambda}\int_{\Omega}\theta^{\lambda}(t)+C(\delta,\lambda,T,\Omega,g,\bm{v}_{0},\mathbb{B}_{0},\theta_{0}),

(2.36)

which, thanks to (
1.15
), (
2.16
) and the Hölder inequality, implies that

\int_{Q_{T}}\frac{|\nabla\theta|^{2}}{\theta^{\lambda}}\leq\tilde{C}(\delta,\lambda,T,\Omega,g,\bm{v}_{0},\mathbb{B}_{0},\theta_{0})\quad\mbox{for all }\lambda\in(1,2).

(2.37)

The estimate (
2.37
) gives that
\nabla(\theta^{\frac{2-\lambda}{2}})
is bounded in
L^{2}(Q_{T})
for any
\lambda\in(1,2)
, and since
\theta
is bounded in
L^{\infty}(0,T;L^{1}(\Omega))
, we have that (
\hat{C}
depends on
\delta,\lambda
,
T
,
\Omega
,
g
,
\bm{v}_{0}
,
\mathbb{B}_{0}
and
\theta_{0}
)

\|\theta^{\frac{2-\lambda}{2}}\|_{L^{\infty}\left(0,T;L^{\frac{2}{2-\lambda}}(\Omega)\right)}+\|\theta^{\frac{2-\lambda}{2}}\|_{L^{2}(0,T;W^{1,2}(\Omega))}\leq\hat{C}\quad\mbox{for all }\lambda\in(1,2).

(2.38)

Hence, since
W^{1,2}(\Omega)\hookrightarrow L^{6}(\Omega)
, we have that
\theta^{\frac{2-\lambda}{2}}
is bounded in
L^{\beta}(Q_{T})
if, for a suitable
\alpha\in(0,1)
, it holds

\frac{\alpha}{\infty}+\frac{1-\alpha}{2}=\frac{1}{\beta},\qquad\frac{\alpha}{\frac{2}{2-\lambda}}+\frac{1-\alpha}{6}=\frac{1}{\beta}.

(2.39)

The equalities in (
2.39
) hold true for

\alpha=\frac{2}{8-3\lambda},\qquad\beta=\frac{2(8-3\lambda)}{6-3\lambda}.

(2.40)

Thus, for all
\lambda\in(1,\frac{5}{3}]
, we have that

\|\theta^{{\frac{2-\lambda}{2}}}\|_{\beta,Q_{T}}=\|\theta\|_{\frac{8-3\lambda}{3},Q_{T}}^{\frac{8-3\lambda}{3\beta}}\leq\overline{C}(\delta,\lambda,T,\Omega,g,\bm{v}_{0},\mathbb{B}_{0},\theta_{0}),

(2.41)

which means that
\|\theta\|_{\frac{5}{3}-\tilde{\delta},Q_{T}}
is bounded for any
\tilde{\delta}\in(0,\frac{2}{3}]
.

To complete the proof of (
2.18
), we use the Young inequality and write, for any
\lambda\in(1,\frac{5}{3}]
, that

\displaystyle\int_{Q_{T}}|\nabla\theta|^{\mu}=\int_{Q_{T}}\left(\frac{|\nabla\theta|^{2}}{\theta^{\lambda}}\right)^{\frac{\mu}{2}}\theta^{\frac{\mu\lambda}{2}}\leq\int_{Q_{T}}\frac{|\nabla\theta|^{2}}{\theta^{\lambda}}+\theta^{\frac{\mu\lambda}{2\sigma}}=\int_{Q_{T}}\frac{|\nabla\theta|^{2}}{\theta^{\lambda}}+\theta^{\frac{2-\lambda}{2}\beta},

(2.42)

where
\mu,\sigma
are such that

\frac{\mu}{2}+\sigma=1,\qquad\frac{\mu\lambda}{2\sigma}=\frac{2-\lambda}{2}\beta,

(2.43)

or equivalently,

\sigma=\frac{2\lambda}{2\lambda+\beta(2-\lambda)}=\frac{3\lambda}{8},\qquad\mu=\frac{2\beta(2-\lambda)}{2\lambda+\beta(2-\lambda)}=\frac{8-3\lambda}{4}.

(2.44)

Let us note that the last expression in (
2.42
) is bounded due to (
2.37
) and (
2.41
). Thus (
2.42
) together with the expression for
\mu
in (
2.44
) give the boundedness of
\|\nabla\theta\|_{\frac{5}{4}-\tilde{\delta},Q_{T}}
for any
\tilde{\delta}\in(0,\frac{1}{4}]
, which completes the proof of (
2.18
).

Last, we prove (
2.19
). The estimate of
\|\ln\theta(t)\|_{1}
independent of
t\in(0,T)
follows from (
2.25
) and the fact that
\ln y\leq y
for any
y\in\mathbb{R}
, i.e., we have (
C
depends on
\mathbb{B}_{0}
and
\theta_{0}
,
\tilde{C}
depends on
\bm{v}_{0}
,
\mathbb{B}_{0}
and
\theta_{0}
)

\|\ln\theta(t)\|_{1}=\int_{\Omega}((\ln\theta(t))_{+}+(\ln\theta(t))_{-})\leq 2\|\theta(t)\|_{1}+C\leq\tilde{C},

(2.45)

where, for any function
f:\mathbb{R}\to\mathbb{R}
, we define

f(y)_{+}:=\max\{0,f(y)\},\quad f(y)_{-}:=\max\{0,-f(y)\},\qquad y\in\mathbb{R}.

(2.46)

Moreover, from (
1.15
), (
2.16
), (
2.24
) integrated over time together with (
1.12
), the nonnegativity of
g^{\prime}(\theta)\tilde{\psi}(\mathbb{B})
and the fact that
\ln\theta\leq\theta
in
Q_{T}
, we deduce that

\|\nabla\ln\theta\|_{2,Q_{T}}=\left\|\frac{\nabla\theta}{\theta}\right\|_{2,Q_{T}}\leq C(\bm{v}_{0},\mathbb{B}_{0},\theta_{0}).

(2.47)

To estimate
|\ln\det\mathbb{B}(t)|
in
L^{2}(\Omega)
independently of
t\in(0,T)
, we first multiply (
1.31
) scalarly by
2\mathbb{F}^{-T}
and use (
1.1
) and (
1.9
) to obtain

\partial_{t}\ln\det\mathbb{B}+\diver((\ln\det\mathbb{B})\bm{v})+\tau\mbox{tr}(\mathbb{B}-\mathbb{I})=0.

(2.48)

Multiplying the equation (
2.48
) by
\ln\det\mathbb{B}
, integrating the result over
(0,t)\times\Omega
, where
t\in(0,T)
is arbitrary, and using the Hölder inequality and (
1.12
), we get

\int_{\Omega}(\ln\det\mathbb{B}(t))^{2}\leq\int_{\Omega}(\ln\det\mathbb{B}_{0})^{2}+C\|\ln\det\mathbb{B}\|_{2,Q_{T}}\|\mathbb{B}-\mathbb{I}\|_{2,Q_{T}}.

Applying
\sup_{t\in(0,T)}
on the left-hand side and the Young inequality on the right-hand side, we obtain that

\sup_{t\in(0,T)}\|\ln\det\mathbb{B}(t)\|_{2}^{2}\leq\|\ln\det\mathbb{B}_{0}\|_{2}^{2}+\varepsilon\sup_{t\in(0,T)}\|\ln\det\mathbb{B}(t)\|_{2}^{2}+\tilde{C}\|\mathbb{B}-\mathbb{I}\|_{2,Q_{T}}^{2}

with
\varepsilon=\varepsilon(T)
sufficiently small and
\tilde{C}=\tilde{C}(\varepsilon)
, which, together with (
2.17
) and (
1.9
), gives

\sup_{t\in(0,T)}\|\ln\det\mathbb{B}(t)\|_{2}\leq\hat{C}(\delta,T,\Omega,g,\bm{v}_{0},\mathbb{B}_{0},\theta_{0}).

(2.49)

This estimate together with (
2.45
) and (
2.47
), yields (
2.19
).

∎

Corollary 2.8
.

Let the assumptions of Proposition
2.7
be satisfied. Then, for
\delta\in(0,1)
introduced in (
1.19
) and any
\tilde{\delta}>0
, there holds

\displaystyle\|\partial_{t}\bm{v}\|_{L^{\frac{4}{3}}(0,T;(W_{\bm{n},\diver}^{1,2})^{*})}+\|\partial_{t}\mathbb{F}\|_{L^{\frac{4}{3}}(0,T;((W^{1,2}(\Omega))^{3\times 3})^{*})}

\displaystyle\leq C(\delta,T,\Omega,g,\bm{v}_{0},\mathbb{B}_{0},\theta_{0}),

(2.50)

\displaystyle\|\partial_{t}\mathbb{B}\|_{L^{1}(0,T;((W^{1,3+\tilde{\delta}}(\Omega))^{3\times 3})^{*})}

\displaystyle\leq C(\delta,\tilde{\delta},T,\Omega,g,\bm{v}_{0},\mathbb{B}_{0},\theta_{0}),

(2.51)

\displaystyle\|\partial_{t}e\|_{L^{1}(0,T;(W^{1,10+\tilde{\delta}}(\Omega))^{*})}

\displaystyle\leq C(\delta,\tilde{\delta},T,\Omega,g,\bm{v}_{0},\mathbb{B}_{0},\theta_{0}).

(2.52)

Proof.

Let us note that the assumptions of Proposition
2.7
imply that the assumptions of Proposition
1.1
and Lemma
2.6
are also fulfilled. To prove (
2.50
) and (
2.51
), we multiply (
1.2
) by a
\bm{w}\in L^{4}(0,T;W_{\bm{n},\diver}^{1,2})
, (
1.31
) by an
\mathbb{A}\in L^{4}(0,T;(W^{1,2}(\Omega))^{3\times 3})
and (
1.3
), which is satisfied thanks to Proposition
1.1
, by an
\tilde{\mathbb{A}}\in L^{\infty}(0,T;(W^{1,3+\tilde{\delta}}(\Omega))^{3\times 3})
, use (
1.7
), integrate all three resulting equations over
Q_{T}
and use the integration by parts to obtain

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{T}\langle\partial_{t}\bm{v},\bm{w}\rangle

\displaystyle=\int_{Q_{T}}(\bm{v}\otimes\bm{v}):\nabla\bm{w}-\int_{Q_{T}}(2\nu\mathbb{D}+g(\theta)\mathbb{B}):\nabla\bm{w}-\int_{\Sigma_{T}}\bm{v}_{\tau}\cdot\bm{w}_{\tau},

(2.53)

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{T}\langle\partial_{t}\mathbb{F},\mathbb{A}\rangle

\displaystyle=\int_{Q_{T}}(\mathbb{F}\otimes\bm{v}):\nabla\mathbb{A}+\int_{Q_{T}}\left(\nabla\bm{v}\mathbb{F}-\frac{\tau}{2}(\mathbb{F}\mathbb{F}^{T}\mathbb{F}-\mathbb{F})\right):\mathbb{A},

(2.54)

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{T}\langle\partial_{t}\mathbb{B},\tilde{\mathbb{A}}\rangle

\displaystyle=\int_{Q_{T}}(\mathbb{B}\otimes\bm{v}):\nabla\tilde{\mathbb{A}}+\int_{Q_{T}}\left(\nabla\bm{v}\mathbb{B}+\mathbb{B}(\nabla\bm{v})^{T}-\tau(\mathbb{B}^{2}-\mathbb{B})\right):\tilde{\mathbb{A}}.

(2.55)

The estimates (
2.16
) and (
2.17
) together with standard Sobolev embeddings and interpolation imply that

\|\bm{v}\|_{L^{2}(0,T;(L^{6}(\Omega))^{3})}+\|\bm{v}\|_{L^{4}(0,T;(L^{3}(\Omega))^{3})}+\|\bm{v}\|_{\frac{10}{3},Q_{T}}\leq C(\delta,T,\Omega,\bm{v}_{0},\mathbb{B}_{0},\theta_{0}).

(2.56)

Then, (
2.50
) and (
2.51
) are direct consequences of (
1.9
), (
2.17
), (
2.53
)–(
2.56
), the embeddings
W^{1,2}(\Omega)\hookrightarrow L^{4}(\Omega)
and
W^{1,3+\tilde{\delta}}(\Omega)\hookrightarrow L^{\infty}(\Omega)
, the boundedness of
g(\theta)
(and
\nu
,
\tau
), the trace theorem and the Hölder inequality.

To prove (
2.52
), we multiply (
1.4
) by a
\varphi\in L^{\infty}(0,T;W^{1,10+\tilde{\delta}}(\Omega))
, use (
1.7
), (
1.8
) and (
1.21
), and integrate the result by parts over
Q_{T}
to get

\begin{split}\int_{0}^{T}\langle\partial_{t}e,\varphi\rangle=\int_{Q_{T}}\left(\theta+(g(\theta)-\theta g^{\prime}(\theta))\tilde{\psi}(\mathbb{B})\right)\bm{v}\cdot\nabla\varphi-\int_{Q_{T}}\kappa\nabla\theta\cdot\nabla\varphi\\
+\int_{Q_{T}}(2\nu|\mathbb{D}|^{2}+g(\theta)\mathbb{B}:\mathbb{D})\varphi.\end{split}

(2.57)

Since
W^{1,10+\tilde{\delta}}(\Omega)\hookrightarrow L^{\infty}(\Omega)
, the last integral on the right-hand side is uniformly bounded thanks to (
1.9
) and (
2.17
) (use also the boundedness of
\nu
and
g(\theta)
and the Hölder inequality). The first integral on the right-hand side of (
2.57
) is uniformly bounded due to (
1.9
), (
2.17
), (
2.18
), (
2.19
), (
1.21
), the facts that
\bm{v}
is uniformly bounded in
(L^{\frac{10}{3}}(Q_{T}))^{3}
(see (
2.56
)) and
g(\theta)-\theta g^{\prime}(\theta)
is uniformly bounded in
L^{\infty}(Q_{T})
(as it is nonnegative and
g
is nondecreasing and bounded) and the Hölder inequality. The uniform boundedness of the second integral on the right-hand side follows from (
2.18
).
∎

2.2
Weak sequential stability

Let
\{(\bm{v}_{k},p_{k},\mathbb{F}_{k},\mathbb{B}_{k},e_{k},\theta_{k})\}_{k\in\mathbb{N}}
be smooth solutions to (
1.1
)–(
1.2
), (
1.31
), (
1.4
)–(
1.12
) with the initial conditions
\bm{v}_{0}
,
\mathbb{F}_{0}
,
\mathbb{B}_{0}
,
e_{0}
,
\theta_{0}
same for all
k\in\mathbb{N}
. Let the material constants be equal to one, let
\bm{f}\equiv\bm{0}
. Let the functions
\nu,\tau,\kappa:\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{R}^{3\times 3}\to\mathbb{R}
and
g:\mathbb{R}\to\mathbb{R}
satisfy the assumptions of Proposition
2.7
. Then, thanks to Proposition
2.7
and Corollary
2.8
, there exist functions
\bm{v}
,
\mathbb{F}
,
e
,
\theta
such that, for a suitably extracted subsequence of
\{\bm{v}_{k},\mathbb{F}_{k},e_{k},\theta_{k}\}_{k\in\mathbb{N}}
, which we do not relabel, and any
\tilde{\delta}>0
, there holds as
k\to\infty
(here, for
q>1
, the symbol
q)
stands for any
\tilde{q}\in[1,q)
)

\displaystyle\bm{v}_{k}

\displaystyle\rightharpoonup^{*}\bm{v}

\displaystyle\mbox{weakly-* in }L^{\infty}(0,T;L^{2}_{\bm{n},\diver})\cap L^{2}(0,T;W_{\bm{n},\diver}^{1,2}),

(2.58)

\displaystyle\mathbb{F}_{k}

\displaystyle\rightharpoonup^{*}\mathbb{F}

\displaystyle\mbox{weakly-* in }L^{\infty}(0,T;(L^{2}(\Omega))^{3\times 3})\cap(L^{4}(Q_{T}))^{3\times 3},

(2.59)

\displaystyle\theta_{k}

\displaystyle\rightharpoonup^{*}\theta

\displaystyle\mbox{weakly-* in }L^{\infty}(0,T;L^{1}(\Omega))\cap L^{\frac{5}{4})}(0,T;W^{1,\frac{5}{4})}(\Omega))\cap L^{\frac{5}{3})}(Q_{T}),

(2.60)

\displaystyle\partial_{t}\bm{v}_{k}

\displaystyle\rightharpoonup\ \partial_{t}\bm{v}

\displaystyle\mbox{weakly in }L^{\frac{4}{3}}(0,T;(W_{\bm{n},\diver}^{1,2})^{*}),

(2.61)

\displaystyle\partial_{t}\mathbb{F}_{k}

\displaystyle\rightharpoonup\ \partial_{t}\mathbb{F}

\displaystyle\mbox{weakly in }L^{\frac{4}{3}}(0,T;((W^{1,2}(\Omega))^{3\times 3})^{*}),

(2.62)

\displaystyle\partial_{t}e_{k}

\displaystyle\rightharpoonup^{*}\partial_{t}e

\displaystyle\mbox{weakly-* in }\mathcal{M}([0,T];(W^{1,10+\tilde{\delta}}(\Omega))^{*}).

(2.63)

For various
nonlinear
(not necessarily tensor) quantities
\mathbb{Q}_{k}
(such as
\nabla\bm{v}_{k}\mathbb{F}_{k}
,
\mathbb{F}_{k}\mathbb{F}_{k}^{T}\mathbb{F}_{k}
,
|\mathbb{F}_{k}|^{2}
and some others introduced below), which are uniformly bounded in some Lebesgue spaces
L^{r}(Q_{T})
,
r\geq 1
, there is an element
\overline{\mathbb{Q}}\in L^{r}(Q_{T})
for
r>1
,
\overline{\mathbb{Q}}\in\mathcal{M}(\overline{Q_{T}})
for
r=1
, and a subsequence of
\{\mathbb{Q}_{k}\}_{k\in\mathbb{N}}
(denoted again
\{\mathbb{Q}_{k}\}_{k\in\mathbb{N}}
) so that the following holds as
k\to\infty
:

\displaystyle\mathbb{Q}_{k}

\displaystyle\rightharpoonup\ \overline{\mathbb{Q}}\quad\textrm{ weakly in }L^{r}(Q_{T})

\displaystyle\textrm{ for }r>1,

(2.64)

\displaystyle\mathbb{Q}_{k}

\displaystyle\rightharpoonup^{*}\overline{\mathbb{Q}}\quad\textrm{ weakly-* in }\mathcal{M}(Q_{T})

\displaystyle\textrm{ for }r=1.

(2.65)

Using this convention, and the weak convergence results above together with the Aubin-Lions compactness lemma and the trace theorem, we get that
(\bm{v},\mathbb{F},e,\theta)
satisfies, for all
\bm{w}\in L^{4}(0,T;W_{\bm{n},\diver}^{1,2})
,
\mathbb{A}\in L^{4}(0,T;(W^{1,2}(\Omega))^{3\times 3})
and
\varphi\in C_{c}^{1}((-\infty,T);W^{1,10+\tilde{\delta}}(\Omega))
, the following identities:

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{T}\langle\partial_{t}\bm{v},\bm{w}\rangle

\displaystyle=\int_{Q_{T}}(\bm{v}\otimes\bm{v}):\nabla\bm{w}-\int_{Q_{T}}(2\nu\mathbb{D}+\overline{g(\theta)\mathbb{F}\mathbb{F}^{T}}):\nabla\bm{w}-\int_{\Sigma_{T}}\bm{v}_{\tau}\cdot\bm{w}_{\tau},

(2.66)

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{T}\langle\partial_{t}\mathbb{F},\mathbb{A}\rangle

\displaystyle=\int_{Q_{T}}(\mathbb{F}\otimes\bm{v}):\nabla\mathbb{A}+\int_{Q_{T}}\left(\overline{\nabla\bm{v}\mathbb{F}}-\frac{\tau}{2}(\overline{\mathbb{F}\mathbb{F}^{T}\mathbb{F}}-\mathbb{F})\right):\mathbb{A},

(2.67)

and

\begin{split}-\int_{Q_{T}}e\partial_{t}\varphi-\int_{\Omega}e_{0}\varphi(0)&=\int_{Q_{T}}\left(\theta+\overline{(g(\theta)-\theta g^{\prime}(\theta))\tilde{\psi}(\mathbb{F}\mathbb{F}^{T})}\right)\bm{v}\cdot\nabla\varphi\\
&\hskip 18.49988pt+\int_{Q_{T}}(2\nu\overline{|\mathbb{D}|^{2}}+\overline{g(\theta)\mathbb{F}\mathbb{F}^{T}:\mathbb{D}})\varphi-\int_{Q_{T}}\kappa\nabla\theta\cdot\nabla\varphi.\end{split}

(2.68)

Our aim is to show that the nonlinear terms converge to proper functions, i.e. that we can remove the bars in (
2.66
)–(
2.68
), where the equation corresponding to (
2.68
) holds at least with inequality such that the left-hand side is greater than or equal to the right-hand side (we are not able to prove the equality due to the term
\overline{|\mathbb{D}|^{2}}
acting in (
2.68
)) and that
e
satisfies (
1.8
). To complete this, we have to show that a subsequence of
\{\theta_{k}\}_{k\in\mathbb{N}}
is convergent almost everywhere in
Q_{T}
and that the sequence
\{\mathbb{F}_{k}\}_{k\in\mathbb{N}}
is compact in
(L^{1}(Q_{T}))^{3\times 3}
(we show the compactness of
\{\mathbb{F}_{k}\}_{k\in\mathbb{N}}
in
(L^{2}(Q_{T}))^{3\times 3}
).

2.2.1
Almost everywhere convergence of the temperatures
\{\theta_{k}\}_{k\in\mathbb{N}}

To prove the almost everywhere convergence of (a subsequence of)
\{\theta_{k}\}_{k\in\mathbb{N}}
we shall employ the Div-curl lemma
1.4
. In this subsection all the constants of uniform bounds that depend on the data of our problem are denoted by
C
.

Our first step is to focus on the entropy equation (
1.29
). Recall that

\eta_{k}:=\ln\theta_{k}-g^{\prime}(\theta_{k})\tilde{\psi}(\mathbb{B}_{k}),\quad\tilde{\psi}(\mathbb{B}_{k}):=\mathrm{tr}\ \mathbb{B}_{k}-3-\ln\det\mathbb{B}_{k}.

(2.69)

By (
1.9
) and (
2.17
) we have that

\displaystyle\|\mathrm{tr}\ \mathbb{B}_{k}\|_{2,Q_{T}}^{2}\leq\|\mathbb{F}_{k}\|_{4,Q_{T}}^{4}\leq C,

(2.70)

which, combined with (
2.19
) and the boundedness of
g^{\prime}
, readily implies

\displaystyle\|\eta_{k}\|_{2,Q_{T}}\leq C.

(2.71)

Then, by (
2.71
), (
2.56
) and Hölder’s inequality, we obtain

\displaystyle\|\eta_{k}\,\bm{v}_{k}\|_{\frac{5}{4},Q_{T}}\leq C.

(2.72)

Now denote

\bm{q}_{k}:=\eta_{k}\,\bm{v}_{k}-\kappa\nabla\ln\theta_{k}.

In particular, the bound (
2.72
) together with (
1.15
) and (
2.19
) implies

\displaystyle\|\bm{q}_{k}\|_{\frac{5}{4},Q_{T}}\leq C.

(2.73)

On the other hand, we may utilize the equation (
1.29
), together with the bounds (
1.15
), (
2.25
) and (
2.47
), to deduce

\|\diver_{t,x}(\eta_{k},\bm{q}_{k})\|_{1,Q_{T}}=\|\partial_{t}\eta_{k}+\diver_{x}\bm{q}_{k}\|_{1,Q_{T}}\\
=\left\|\kappa\frac{|\nabla_{x}\theta_{k}|^{2}}{\theta^{2}_{k}}+\nu\frac{|\mathbb{D}_{k}|^{2}}{\theta_{k}}+\tau g(\theta_{k})\frac{|\mathbb{B}_{k}-\mathbb{I}|^{2}}{\theta_{k}}\right\|_{1,Q_{T}}\leq C.

(2.74)

Thus, by (
2.71
), (
2.73
), (
2.74
) and the Sobolev embeddings, there exist some
\overline{\eta}\in L^{\frac{5}{4}}(Q_{T})
and
\overline{\bm{q}}\in(L^{\frac{5}{4}}(Q_{T}))^{3}
such that (up to subsequences)

\displaystyle\eta_{k}

\displaystyle\rightharpoonup\overline{\eta}\quad\text{ weakly in }L^{\frac{5}{4}}(Q_{T}),

(2.75)

\displaystyle\bm{q}_{k}

\displaystyle\rightharpoonup\overline{\bm{q}}\quad\text{ weakly in }(L^{\frac{5}{4}}(Q_{T}))^{3},

(2.76)

\displaystyle\{\diver_{t,x}(\eta_{k},\bm{q}_{k})\}_{k\in\mathbb{N}}

\displaystyle\text{ is compact in }(W^{1,5}_{0}(Q_{T}))^{*}.

(2.77)

To proceed, we need an explicitly stated equation for
\tilde{\psi}(\mathbb{B}_{k})
. By (
1.1
), (
1.9
) and (
2.21
) we can multiply (
1.31
) scalarly by
2(\mathbb{F}_{k}-\mathbb{F}_{k}^{-1})
to obtain

\partial_{t}\tilde{\psi}(\mathbb{B}_{k})+\diver_{x}(\tilde{\psi}(\mathbb{B}_{k})\bm{v})+\tau|\mathbb{B}_{k}-\mathbb{I}|^{2}=2(\mathbb{B}_{k}-\mathbb{I}):\mathbb{D}_{k}.

Hence, in a similar way, with the use of the equation above, the bounds (
2.19
), (
2.70
), (
2.56
), as well as (
1.14
), (
2.17
) and the Sobolev embeddings, there exist
\overline{\tilde{\psi}(\mathbb{B})}\in L^{\frac{5}{4}}(Q_{T})
such that (up to a subsequence)

\displaystyle\tilde{\psi}(\mathbb{B}_{k})

\displaystyle\rightharpoonup\overline{\tilde{\psi}(\mathbb{B})}\,\,\,\quad\text{ weakly in }L^{\frac{5}{4}}(Q_{T}),

(2.78)

\displaystyle\tilde{\psi}(\mathbb{B}_{k})\,\bm{v}_{k}

\displaystyle\rightharpoonup\overline{\tilde{\psi}(\mathbb{B})}\,\bm{v}\quad\text{ weakly in }(L^{\frac{5}{4}}(Q_{T}))^{3},

(2.79)

\displaystyle\{\diver_{t,x}(\tilde{\psi}(\mathbb{B}_{k}),\tilde{\psi}(\mathbb{B}_{k})\,\bm{v}_{k})\}_{k\in\mathbb{N}}

\displaystyle\text{ is compact in }(W^{1,5}_{0}(Q_{T}))^{*}.

(2.80)

Now consider the sequence
\{\theta_{k}^{\frac{1}{5}}\}_{k\in\mathbb{N}}
. By (
2.18
) there holds

\displaystyle\|\theta_{k}^{\frac{1}{5}}\|_{6,Q_{T}}\leq C,

(2.81)

and by (
2.37
)

\displaystyle\|\nabla_{x}(\theta_{k}^{\frac{1}{5}})\|_{2,Q_{T}}=\frac{1}{5}\|\theta_{k}^{-\frac{4}{5}}\nabla_{x}\theta_{k}\|_{2,Q_{T}}\leq C.

Thus

\displaystyle\|\mathrm{curl}_{t,x}\,(\theta_{k}^{\frac{1}{5}},0,0,0)\|_{2,Q_{T}}\leq\|\nabla_{x}(\theta_{k}^{\frac{1}{5}})\|_{2,Q_{T}}\leq C.

(2.82)

In particular, by (
2.81
), (
2.82
) and the Sobolev embeddings, there exists some
\overline{\theta^{\frac{1}{5}}}\in L^{6}(Q_{T})
such that (up to a subsequence)

\displaystyle\theta_{k}^{\frac{1}{5}}

\displaystyle\rightharpoonup\overline{\theta^{\frac{1}{5}}}\quad\text{ weakly in }L^{6}(Q_{T}),

(2.83)

\displaystyle\{\mathrm{curl}_{t,x}\,(\theta_{k}^{\frac{1}{5}},0,0,0)\}_{k\in\mathbb{N}}

\displaystyle\text{ compact in }((W^{1,2}_{0}(Q_{T}))^{6})^{*}.

(2.84)

We may combine (
2.75
)–(
2.80
), (
2.83
)–(
2.84
) and use the Div-curl lemma
1.4
to deduce

\displaystyle\eta_{k}\,\theta_{k}^{\frac{1}{5}}

\displaystyle\rightharpoonup\overline{\eta}\,\overline{\theta^{\frac{1}{5}}}

\displaystyle\text{ weakly in }L^{1}(Q_{T}),

(2.85)

\displaystyle\tilde{\psi}(\mathbb{B}_{k})\,\theta_{k}^{\frac{1}{5}}

\displaystyle\rightharpoonup\overline{\tilde{\psi}(\mathbb{B})}\,\overline{\theta^{\frac{1}{5}}}

\displaystyle\text{ weakly in }L^{1}(Q_{T}).

(2.86)

Next, let us note that
w\mapsto-g^{\prime}(w)
and
w\mapsto w^{\frac{1}{5}}
are nondecreasing functions. Thus, due to the nonnegativity of
\tilde{\psi}(\mathbb{B}_{k})
, we have in
Q_{T}
for any
w\in\mathbb{R}

0\leq\tilde{\psi}(\mathbb{B}_{k})(-g^{\prime}(\theta_{k})+g^{\prime}(w))(\theta_{k}^{\frac{1}{5}}-w^{\frac{1}{5}}).

In particular, for
w:=\left(\overline{\theta^{\frac{1}{5}}}\right)^{5}
, we get a.e. in
Q_{T}

\displaystyle 0\leq\tilde{\psi}(\mathbb{B}_{k})\left(-g^{\prime}(\theta_{k})+g^{\prime}\left(\left(\overline{\theta^{\frac{1}{5}}}\right)^{5}\right)\right)\left(\theta_{k}^{\frac{1}{5}}-\overline{\theta^{\frac{1}{5}}}\right),

which can be rewritten as follows

\displaystyle-\tilde{\psi}(\mathbb{B}_{k})g^{\prime}(\theta_{k})\overline{\theta^{\frac{1}{5}}}\leq-\tilde{\psi}(\mathbb{B}_{k})g^{\prime}(\theta_{k})\theta_{k}^{\frac{1}{5}}+\tilde{\psi}(\mathbb{B}_{k})g^{\prime}\left(\left(\overline{\theta^{\frac{1}{5}}}\right)^{5}\right)\left(\theta_{k}^{\frac{1}{5}}-\overline{\theta^{\frac{1}{5}}}\right).

Integrating the last inequality over
Q_{T}
, using the boundedness of
g^{\prime}
, the uniform boundedness of
\{\tilde{\psi}(\mathbb{B}_{k})\}_{k\in\mathbb{N}}
in
L^{2}(Q_{T})
and
\{\theta_{k}^{\frac{1}{5}}\}_{k\in\mathbb{N}}
in
L^{6}(Q_{T})
(see (
2.19
), (
2.70
) and (
2.81
)) and the Hölder inequality, taking the limit
k\to\infty
and using the convention (
2.64
), we obtain, due to (
2.86
), that

\displaystyle\int_{Q_{T}}-\overline{g^{\prime}(\theta)\tilde{\psi}(\mathbb{B})}\,\,\,\overline{\theta^{\frac{1}{5}}}\leq\int_{Q_{T}}-\overline{g^{\prime}(\theta)\tilde{\psi}(\mathbb{B})\theta^{\frac{1}{5}}}.

(2.87)

Let us also recall that the sequence
\{\ln\theta_{k}\}_{k\in\mathbb{N}}
is uniformly bounded in
L^{2}(Q_{T})
. Having this and (
2.64
), we may apply (
2.69
), (
2.85
) and (
2.87
) to get

\begin{split}\int_{Q_{T}}\overline{\ln(\theta)}\,\overline{\theta^{\frac{1}{5}}}&=\int_{Q_{T}}\overline{\eta}\,\overline{\theta^{\frac{1}{5}}}+\int_{Q_{T}}\overline{g^{\prime}(\theta)\tilde{\psi}(\mathbb{B})}\,\,\,\overline{\theta^{\frac{1}{5}}}\\
&=\int_{Q_{T}}\overline{\eta\,\theta^{\frac{1}{5}}}+\int_{Q_{T}}\overline{g^{\prime}(\theta)\tilde{\psi}(\mathbb{B})}\,\,\,\overline{\theta^{\frac{1}{5}}}\\
&=\int_{Q_{T}}\overline{\ln(\theta)\theta^{\frac{1}{5}}}-\int_{Q_{T}}\overline{g^{\prime}(\theta)\tilde{\psi}(\mathbb{B})\theta^{\frac{1}{5}}}+\int_{Q_{T}}\overline{g^{\prime}(\theta)\tilde{\psi}(\mathbb{B})}\,\overline{\theta^{\frac{1}{5}}}\\
&\geq\int_{Q_{T}}\overline{\ln(\theta)\theta^{\frac{1}{5}}}.\end{split}

(2.88)

Notice that
w\mapsto\ln(w)
and
w\mapsto w^{\frac{1}{5}}
are increasing functions. Thus, for any
w\in L^{1}(Q_{T})
such that
w>0
a.e. in
Q_{T}
and
\ln w\in L^{\frac{5}{4}}(Q_{T})
, there holds

\displaystyle\int_{Q_{T}}(\ln(\theta_{k})-\ln(w))(\theta_{k}^{\frac{1}{5}}-w^{\frac{1}{5}})\geq 0.

Due to (
2.88
) we may let
k\to\infty
in the above inequality to deduce

\displaystyle\int_{Q_{T}}(\overline{\ln(\theta)}-\ln(w))(\overline{\theta^{\frac{1}{5}}}-w^{\frac{1}{5}})\geq 0.

(2.89)

Now, by (
2.18
), (
2.19
) and the weak lower-semicontinuity of the convex function

\phi\mapsto\int_{Q_{T}}e^{\phi}
, we may in (
2.89
) consider

w:=e^{\overline{\ln\theta}-\lambda h},\quad\lambda>0,h\in L^{\infty}(Q_{T})\text{ - arbitrary}

and obtain

\int_{Q_{T}}(\overline{\theta^{\frac{1}{5}}}-e^{\frac{1}{5}(\overline{\ln\theta}-\lambda h)})h\geq 0.

Letting
\lambda\to 0^{+}
, using Lebesgue’s Dominated Convergence Theorem and the arbitrariness of
h
, we get

\displaystyle\overline{e^{\frac{1}{5}\ln\theta}}=\overline{\theta^{\frac{1}{5}}}=e^{\frac{1}{5}\overline{\ln\theta}}\text{ a.e. in }Q_{T}.

(2.90)

Since
w\mapsto e^{\frac{1}{5}w}
is an increasing function, the above equalities imply that

(\overline{\ln(\theta)}-\ln(\theta_{k}))(\overline{\theta^{\frac{1}{5}}}-\theta_{k}^{\frac{1}{5}})=(\overline{\ln(\theta)}-\ln(\theta_{k}))(e^{\frac{1}{5}\overline{\ln\theta}}-e^{\frac{1}{5}\ln\theta_{k}})\geq 0\text{ a.e. in }Q_{T}.

(2.91)

Now let us set
w:=\theta_{k}
in (
2.89
). Thanks to (
2.88
) and (
2.91
), we obtain

\displaystyle\lim_{k\to+\infty}\int_{Q_{T}}|(\overline{\ln(\theta)}-\ln(\theta_{k}))(\overline{\theta^{\frac{1}{5}}}-\theta_{k}^{\frac{1}{5}})|=\lim_{k\to+\infty}\int_{Q_{T}}(\overline{\ln(\theta)}-\ln(\theta_{k}))(\overline{\theta^{\frac{1}{5}}}-\theta_{k}^{\frac{1}{5}})=0.

Hence, up to a subsequence, there holds

\displaystyle(\overline{\ln(\theta)}-\ln(\theta_{k}))(e^{\frac{1}{5}\overline{\ln\theta}}-e^{\frac{1}{5}\ln\theta_{k}})=(\overline{\ln(\theta)}-\ln(\theta_{k}))(\overline{\theta^{\frac{1}{5}}}-\theta_{k}^{\frac{1}{5}})\rightarrow 0\text{ a.e. in }Q_{T}.

Since
w\mapsto e^{\frac{1}{5}w}
is a strictly increasing function, the above is only possible if

\displaystyle\ln(\theta_{k})\rightarrow\overline{\ln\theta}\text{ a.e. in }Q_{T},

which in turn implies that

\displaystyle\theta_{k}\rightarrow\theta:=e^{\overline{\ln\theta}}\text{ a.e. in }Q_{T}.

(2.92)

2.2.2
Compactness of
\{\mathbb{F}_{k}\}_{k\in\mathbb{N}}
in
(L^{2}(Q_{T}))^{3\times 3}

The proof follows the scheme introduced in
[
32
]
or
[
14
]
. Formally, the evolutionary equation for
\mathbb{F}_{k}
is multiplied scalarly by
\mathbb{F}_{k}
, the limited equation for
\mathbb{F}
is multiplied scalarly by
\mathbb{F}
and we estimate the difference
|\mathbb{F}_{k}|^{2}-|\mathbb{F}|^{2}
. Note that the sequence
\{\bm{v}_{k},|\mathbb{F}_{k}|\}_{k\in\mathbb{N}}
is uniformly bounded in
L^{2}(0,T;W_{\bm{n},\diver}^{1,2})\times L^{4}(Q_{T})
. For the quantity
\overline{|\mathbb{F}|^{2}}-|\mathbb{F}|^{2}
and an arbitrary non-negative
\varphi\in C_{c}^{\infty}((-\infty,T)\times\Omega)
, we aim to get the following Gronwall type inequality

-\int_{Q_{T}}(\overline{|\mathbb{F}|^{2}}-|\mathbb{F}|^{2})\partial_{t}\varphi-\int_{Q_{T}}(\overline{|\mathbb{F}|^{2}}-|\mathbb{F}|^{2})\bm{v}\cdot\nabla\varphi\leq\int_{Q_{T}}L(\overline{|\mathbb{F}|^{2}}-|\mathbb{F}|^{2})\varphi,

(2.93)

where
L
is an
L^{2}(Q_{T})
function specified bellow. Then we apply Proposition 1.5 from
[
14
]
and conclude that
\overline{|\mathbb{F}|^{2}}-|\mathbb{F}|^{2}=0
almost everywhere in
Q_{T}
, which is equivalent to the compactness of
\{\mathbb{F}_{k}\}_{k\in\mathbb{N}}
in
(L^{2}(Q_{T}))^{3\times 3}
, see
[
14
]
.

Let us note that, while deriving (
2.93
), the most complicated terms to handle are
\int_{Q_{T}}\nabla\bm{v}_{k}:\mathbb{F}_{k}\mathbb{F}_{k}^{T}\varphi
, acting in the equation (
1.31
) with
\mathbb{F}_{k}
in the role of
\mathbb{F}
multiplied scalarly by
\varphi\mathbb{F}_{k}
and integrated over
Q_{T}
, and
\int_{Q_{T}}\overline{(\nabla\bm{v})\mathbb{F}}:\mathbb{F}\varphi
, acting in the mollified (by the standard mollifying kernel
\omega_{\hat{\delta}}
,
\hat{\delta}>0
, for more details see
[
13
]
or
[
14
]
) form of (
2.67
) with
\mathbb{A}
being equal to the mollification of
\varphi\mathbb{F}
, where in the equation with the mollified terms the limit in the mollifying parameter
\hat{\delta}\to 0+
is taken. To treat the mentioned terms, we shall employ, among other things, the evolutionary equation for
\bm{v}_{k}
multiplied scalarly by
\bm{v}_{k}-\bm{v}
.
However, in this equation, the convective term

(\bm{v}_{k}-\bm{v})\diver(\bm{v}_{k}\otimes\bm{v}_{k})
is not uniformly bounded in
(L^{1}(Q_{T}))^{3}
. To avoid this obstacle we regularize the equation for
\mathbb{F}_{k}
by
G_{m}(|\mathbb{F}_{k}|^{2})
, where
G_{m}(s)
is a cut-off function defined, for
m\in\mathbb{N}
, as

G_{m}(s)=G(s/m),\quad G(s)=\left\{\begin{matrix}1&\mbox{if }|s|\leq 1\\
0&\mbox{if }|s|>2\end{matrix}\right.,\quad G\in C^{\infty}(\mathbb{R})

(2.94)

such that
G
is nonincreasing in
[0,\infty)
,
and in the equation for
\bm{v}_{k}
we use the tools connected with the existence of biting limits, namely Propositions
1.2
and
1.3
. Note that such a procedure was already used in the analysis of the Giesekus model in the isothermal three-dimensional setting, see
[
14
]
.

The proof of the compactness of
\{\mathbb{F}_{k}\}_{k\in\mathbb{N}}
in
(L^{2}(Q_{T}))^{3\times 3}
consists of two main steps.

Step 1.

Subtracting the mollified form of (
2.67
) with
\mathbb{A}
being the mollification of
\varphi\mathbb{F}
from (
1.31
) with
\mathbb{F}_{k}
in the role of
\mathbb{F}
multiplied scalarly by
\varphi G_{m}(|\mathbb{F}_{k}|^{2})\mathbb{F}_{k}
and integrated over
Q_{T}
, and taking (besides the limit in the mollifying parameter) the limits
k\to\infty
and
m\to\infty
, we shall get, after certain arrangements, the following inequality, valid for all non-negative
\varphi\in\mathcal{C}_{c}^{\infty}((-\infty,T)\times\Omega)
:

\begin{split}-&\int_{Q_{T}}\left(\overline{|\mathbb{F}|^{2}}-|\mathbb{F}|^{2}\right)\partial_{t}\varphi-\int_{Q_{T}}\left(\overline{|\mathbb{F}|^{2}}-|\mathbb{F}|^{2}\right)\bm{v}\cdot\nabla\varphi\\
